L11: Application Type	NDA
Application Number(s)	216993
Priority or Standard	Priority
Submit Date(s)	August 24, 2022
Received Date(s)	August 24, 2022
PDUFA Goal Date	July 24, 2023
Division/Office	DHM1/OOD
Review Completion Date	July 19, 2023
Established Name	Quizartinib
(Proposed) Trade Name	Vanflyta
Pharmacologic Class	Kinase inhibitor
Code name	Quizartinib dihydrochloride [1-(5-tert-butyl-1,2-oxazol-3-yl)-3-(4-{7-[2-(morpholin-4-yl)ethoxy]imidazo[2,1-b][1,3]benzothiazol-2-yl}phenyl)urea dihydrochloride]
Applicant	Daiichi Sankyo, Inc.
Formulation(s)	Tablets
Dosing Regimen	Vanflyta should be administered in combination with standard chemotherapy at a dose of 35.4 mg once daily for two weeks in each cycle of induction. See Section 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization for additional information.
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	In combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy, and as continuation monotherapy following consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 internal tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) positive as detected by an FDA-approved test.
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Regular Approval
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	In combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and cytarabine consolidation, and as maintenance monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed acute

L64: ## 1 Executive Summary
L65: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L66: Proposed Trade Name:	Vanflyta
Established Name:	Quizartinib
Therapeutic Class:	Antineoplastic
Chemical Class:	Small molecule
Pharmacologic Class:	Kinase inhibitor
Dosage Forms:	Oral tablet (17.7 mg and 26.5 mg)
L67: Quizartinib is a new molecular entity, an orally administered, small molecule kinase inhibitor. Quizartinib was previously evaluated under NDA 212166 for the treatment of adults with relapsed or refractory acute myeloid leukemia (AML) which is FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 internal tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) positive, as detected by an FDA-approved test. However, that application received a Complete Response on June 14, 2019, due to several concerns, including failure to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness and serious risks including fatal cardiac events. The current NDA 216993 was submitted for a newly proposed indication in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy, and as continuation monotherapy following consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed AML that is FLT3-ITD positive as detected by an FDA-approved test. The proposed dosage is 35.4 mg (40 mg salt) orally once daily on Days 8 to 21 of each cycle of induction (or Days 6 to 19 for Induction cycle 2) with cytarabine and anthracycline and on Days 6 to 19 of each cycle of consolidation with high-dose cytarabine. Following induction and consolidation, the proposed dosage of quizartinib as maintenance monotherapy is 26.5 mg (30 mg salt) once daily Days 1 through 14 of the 28-day cycle, and if the QT interval corrected by Fridericia's formula (QTcF) is less than or equal to 450 msec, the dosage is increased to 53 mg (60 mg salt) once daily for the remainder of the 28-day cycle; maintenance therapy may be continued until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity for up to thirty-six 28-day cycles.
L68: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L69: The review team recommends regular approval for quizartinib "in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and cytarabine consolidation, and as maintenance monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is FLT3 internal tandem duplication (ITD)-positive as detected by an FDA-approved test." The recommendation is based on the findings of improved overall survival (OS) in Study AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First). Supportive evidence
L70: was also demonstrated with data on complete remission (CR) and CR with partial hematologic recovery (CRh) rates from Study AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R).
L71: Quizartinib was evaluated in one randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase 3 study in adult patients ages 18 to 75 years with newly diagnosed AML with FLT3-ITD mutation. Patients were randomized to quizartinib (n=268) or placebo (n=271) in combination with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy, followed by a maintenance monotherapy phase per the initial randomization for up to 36 months. Randomization was stratified by age, white blood count at diagnosis, and region. The demographic and disease characteristics of the randomized patients were balanced between arms.
L72: The final analysis of QuANTUM-First included a prespecified assessment of the primary endpoint of OS, measured from the date of randomization until death from any cause. At the time of analysis, there was an OS advantage in patients who received quizartinib in addition to standard chemotherapy compared to those who received placebo in combination with standard chemotherapy with HR 0.78 (95% confidence interval [CI] 0.62, 0.98; p=0.0324).
L73: The improvement in OS on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm versus the placebo + chemotherapy arm was generally consistent across various disease subsets (e.g., intermediate cytogenetics HR 0.76, unfavorable cytogenetics HR 0.45, FLT3-ITD variant allelic frequency [VAF] \ge 3 to \le 25\% HR 0.87, FLT3-ITD VAF &gt;25\% to \le 50\% HR 0.77, and FLT3-ITD VAF &gt;50\% HR 0.53). Patients with favorable cytogenetics and NPM1 wild-type patients, however, had HR 2.07 (95% CI 0.71-5.97) and HR 1.01 (95% CI 0.73-1.41), respectively. Given the small sample size for the favorable cytogenetic risk group (N=14 quizartinib + chemotherapy, N=19 placebo + chemotherapy) and the post-hoc nature of these analyses, no firm conclusions can be drawn.
L74: Secondary endpoints on QuANTUM-First included event-free survival (EFS) and CR rate. EFS was defined as the time from randomization until the date of the earliest of refractory disease or treatment failure determined at the end of the Induction Phase (ITF), relapse, or death from any cause. ITF was defined as failure to achieve CR within 42 days of the start of the last cycle of induction. At the time of the final analysis, EFS was not significantly improved on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm (HR 0.92, 95% CI 0.75-1.11; p-value 0.2371).
L75: FDA-adjudicated CRs were achieved by 147 (55%) patients on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm and 150 (55%) patients on the placebo + chemotherapy arm. The median duration of CR (DOCR) was 38.6 (95% CI, 21.9 - not evaluable) months on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm and was 12.4 (95% CI, 8.8-22.7) months on the placebo + chemotherapy arm.
L76: Following completion of consolidation therapy and/or hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT), maintenance therapy (termed “continuation” therapy in the protocol) was initiated by 39% of patients on QuANTUM-First. FDA guidance has been to rerandomize prior to
L77: maintenance therapy to allow for isolation of the treatment effect of study drugs on maintenance (FDA's Guidance for Industry Acute Myeloid Leukemia: Developing Drugs and Biological Products for Treatment, 2022). While rerandomization was not performed in QuANTUM-First and the contribution of treatment effects of quizartinib from different phases of therapy cannot be certain, the clinical data demonstrated a later separation in the survival curves and relatively compelling results on OS in patients *who received maintenance post-consolidation chemotherapy*, such that limiting the indication for quizartinib to induction and consolidation therapy may limit the efficacy of the regimen in this patient population (see Section 8.1).
L78: The review team recommends a limitation of use that quizartinib "is not indicated as maintenance monotherapy following allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT); improvement in overall survival with [quizartinib] in this setting has not been demonstrated." The applicant did not establish safety and effectiveness of quizartinib in patients *who received maintenance post-HSCT*, a biologically distinct subgroup of patients with different benefit-risk considerations. The post-HSCT population has received a known potentially curative therapy, which may preclude any further benefit from a prolonged maintenance regimen, and these patients are prone to additional risks, particularly infectious risks, that make the benefit-risk considerations distinct in this patient subgroup. Furthermore, the design and conduct of the trial was inadequate to support an evaluation of the benefit-risk in this subgroup given the relatively small sample size and post-hoc exploratory nature utilizing post-randomization subgroup comparisons. The limited data that are available from the QuANTUM-First trial in this patient population suggest that use of quizartinib in the maintenance setting may add toxicity without added benefit (see Sections 8.1 &amp; 8.2). Recent results also showed lack of benefit of gilteritinib, another FLT3-inhibitor, that was studied in a large randomized, controlled maintenance therapy trial post-HSCT (Astellas and BMT CTN Announce Topline Results from Phase 3 MORPHO Trial of Gilteritinib, 2023).
L79: Efficacy of quizartinib was supplemented by data from Study QuANTUM-R. QuANTUM-R was an open-label, randomized (2:1), controlled, multicenter clinical trial of quizartinib monotherapy versus choice of salvage chemotherapy in patients with relapsed or refractory (R/R) FLT3-ITD positive AML. The trial randomized 367 adult patients, including 245 patients on the quizartinib arm, with a target dose of 53 mg orally once daily. The primary endpoint was OS, and the study showed a statistically positive result with hazard ratio [HR] 0.77 (95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.59-0.99; p = 0.019; median OS 26.9 weeks vs 20.4 weeks). However, there was lack of internal consistency in other efficacy endpoints (event-free survival HR 0.90, CR rate 4% quizartinib versus 1% chemotherapy), an imbalance in use of HSCT favoring the quizartinib arm, and differences between arms in patients randomized and not treated and early censored that favored the quizartinib arm, leading to the lack of a robust analysis of OS. These uncertain efficacy results contributed to the Complete Response action on NDA 212166. However, the QuANTUM-R study demonstrated quantifiable single-agent activity of quizartinib compared to
L80: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L81: the active control arm. On the quizartinib arm, the CR plus CRh rate was 11% and transfusion independence (TI) was achieved by 26% of patients for at least 56 days post-baseline. Thus, while the prior review concluded that QuANTUM-R was not an adequate and well-controlled trial to support a separate indication, the study provides supportive evidence of clinical benefit for quizartinib in patients with FLT3-ITD positive AML.
L82: The FDA review team concludes that the overall survival benefit of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy in patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD mutated AML, supported by CR+CRh rates with quizartinib monotherapy in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD positive AML constitute substantial evidence of effectiveness. However, based on inadequate evidence to establish a favorable benefit-risk profile for quizartinib maintenance therapy post HSCT, the indication will only specify use of maintenance quizartinib following consolidation chemotherapy and will include a limitation of use that “[quizartinib] is not indicated as maintenance monotherapy following allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT);
L83: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L84: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L85: Quizartinib is an oral Class III receptor tyrosine kinase (RTK) inhibitor that binds to FLT3 and several other Class III RTKs, with a proposed indication in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy, and as continuation monotherapy following consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed AML that is FLT3-ITD positive as detected by an FDA-approved test. Quizartinib was previously evaluated under NDA 212166 for monotherapy treatment of adults with relapsed or refractory FLT3-ITD positive AML and received a Complete Response on June 14, 2019. This new NDA is seeking an indication for quizartinib in combination with standard chemotherapy in patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML.
L86: FLT3-ITD positive AML is an aggressive form of AML associated with increased relapse rates and inferior survival. Standard of care for eligible patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML includes treatment with induction and consolidation chemotherapy in combination with the FDA-approved kinase inhibitor midostaurin. Midostaurin was approved based on a randomized, placebo-controlled trial comparing midostaurin plus chemotherapy versus placebo plus chemotherapy in patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-mutated AML ages 18-60 years. Although the indication was not restricted to adults \le 60 years, the lack of enrollment of older patients on the pivotal study led to some uncertainty regarding treatment of older patients with chemotherapy plus midostaurin. Furthermore, long-term outcomes in patients with FLT3-ITD mutated AML continue to be suboptimal, leaving this patient population with a significant unmet medical need.
L87: The recommendation for regular approval is based on the efficacy results from one randomized, controlled trial evaluating quizartinib in combination with standard chemotherapy in patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML, with supportive data from a randomized, controlled trial evaluating quizartinib monotherapy in patients with relapsed or refractory FLT3-ITD positive AML. Study QuANTUM-First was a randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled trial of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy (n=268) versus placebo in combination with chemotherapy (n=271). Randomization was stratified by age, white blood count at diagnosis, and region. Patients randomized to quizartinib plus chemotherapy had significantly longer survival compared to placebo plus chemotherapy (HR 0.78; 95% CI: 0.62 – 0.98; p-value = 0.032). The HR for EFS was 0.92 and CR rate was 55% on both arms. Median DOCR was 38.6 months (95% CI, 21.9 to not evaluable) and 12.4 months (95% CI, 8.8 to 22.7), respectively. Analysis of OS in patients treated with post-consolidation maintenance
L88: suggested a benefit to maintenance monotherapy with quizartinib (HR 0.68), but this was driven by patients who received maintenance post-consolidation chemotherapy (HR 0.40), with no apparent benefit in patients post-HSCT (HR 1.62).
L89: Study QuANTUM-R was an open-label, randomized (2:1), controlled, multicenter clinical trial of quizartinib monotherapy versus choice of salvage chemotherapy in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD positive AML. The trial served as the basis of the initial NDA 212166 submitted for quizartinib. The trial randomized 367 patients, including 245 on the quizartinib arm, with a target dose of 53 mg daily. OS was statistically positive with a HR 0.77 (95% CI 0.59, 0.99; p=0.019), but issues with the study's conduct called into question the robustness of the OS results. The CR+CRh rate for quizartinib was 11%, with TI achieved by 26% for at least 56 days post-baseline. Thus, quizartinib had measurable, albeit low, single agent activity in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD positive AML.
L90: Safety of quizartinib was demonstrated on the QuANTUM-First study, as well as in the Applicant's Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS) AML population, which included the quizartinib arm of QuANTUM-R and several single-arm studies of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy and as monotherapy in patients with AML. Common adverse reactions (&gt;30\%) not related to a laboratory evaluation for the combination of quizartinib plus chemotherapy from Study QuANTUM-First were febrile neutropenia, diarrhea, mucositis, and nausea. Frequent serious adverse reactions (&gt;5\%) included febrile neutropenia. QT interval prolongation occurred in 14% of patients on the quizartinib arm, with 3% being Grade 3-4 in severity. Of 1,081 patients in the ISS AML population, torsades de pointes occurred in 0.2%, cardiac arrest occurred in 0.6% (0.4% with a fatal outcome), and 0.1% experienced ventricular fibrillation. Adverse reactions were mitigated by electrocardiogram (ECG) monitoring, electrolyte monitoring and correction, anti-infectives, and transfusions as indicated. The seriousness of the QT prolongation and arrhythmia risk warrants a boxed warning and specific instructions to patients regarding the risks and optimal management. Furthermore, it was determined that a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) with Elements to Assure Safe Use (ETASU) was necessary to educate prescribers of the unique and potentially fatal risk of arrhythmias, as well as the risk factors for, and strategies to mitigate QT prolongation, torsades de pointe, and fatal cardiac arrest.
L91: In terms of safety in the maintenance phase on QuANTUM-First, common adverse reactions in quizartinib-treated patients during maintenance included neutropenia, gastrointestinal toxicity (diarrhea, nausea, abdominal pain, vomiting, decreased appetite), thrombocytopenia, upper respiratory infections, herpesvirus infections, mucositis, hypertransaminasemia, QTc prolonged, musculoskeletal pain, cough, and headache. Most of the reported adverse reactions during this phase occurred during the first year of maintenance treatment. Notably, patients who received maintenance therapy post-HSCT had a higher rate of adverse reactions to quizartinib (on average 8% higher) than for patients who received quizartinib maintenance post-consolidation chemotherapy without HSCT. Thus, the safety of extended maintenance therapy with
L92: quizartinib for 3 years post-HSCT was deemed unacceptable in the face of the possible OS detriment in this subgroup and lack of benefit of gilteritinib, another FLT3 inhibitor, in a properly conducted randomized, controlled trial in the post-HSCT maintenance setting (Astellas and BMT CTN Announce Topline Results from Phase 3 MORPHO Trial of Gilteritinib, 2023).
L93: In conclusion, the primary endpoint used to assess efficacy in the pivotal QuANTUM-First trial was OS. The median OS was 31.9 months (95% CI 21.8, not evaluable) on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm compared to 15.1 months (95% CI 13.2, 26.8) on the placebo + chemotherapy arm with HR 0.78 (95% CI 0.62, 0.98); p = 0.0324. Analyses to discern the impact of the maintenance phase of quizartinib monotherapy on the OS results suggested a positive effect overall (HR 0.68), but there was inadequate evidence to establish a favorable benefit-risk profile in the subgroup of patients who received maintenance post-HSCT (HR 1.62 and added toxicity), leading to inclusion of only post-consolidation chemotherapy maintenance in the indication statement and a limitation of use for maintenance monotherapy post-HSCT. These findings were supported by external information from the results of the dedicated post-HSCT randomized placebo-controlled maintenance clinical trial of gilteritinib. Results of QuANTUM-R showing HR for OS 0.77 for quizartinib monotherapy versus choice of salvage chemotherapy, CR+CRh rate of 11%, and transfusion independence 26% support the effectiveness of quizartinib in patients with FLT3-ITD positive AML.
L94: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	FLT3-ITD positive AML is a fatal disease
Long-term prognosis with available therapy remains suboptimal
AML is a fatal disease.
Patients have poor long-term outcomes despite available therapies.
Current Treatment Options	Patients with newly-diagnosed AML with positive FLT3-ITD who are eligible for treatment with intensive chemotherapy may use midostaurin in combination with chemotherapy with reported CR rates of 59%. The median OS was approximately 75 months. Of note, the pivotal study for midostaurin only enrolled patients ages 18-60 years.
There is a need for effective therapies for patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML who are eligible for intensive induction chemotherapy.
L95: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	QuANTUM-First was a randomized (1:1), placebo-controlled trial that randomized 268 patients ages 18-75 years to quizartinib in combination with induction and consolidation chemotherapy, followed by quizartinib maintenance, and 271 patients to placebo in combination with induction and consolidation chemotherapy, followed by placebo maintenance.
Median OS on QuANTUM-First was significantly longer on the quizartinib versus the placebo arm (HR 0.78; 95% CI: 0.62 – 0.98; p-value = 0.032). EFS was not significantly improved (HR 0.92; p-value = 0.23) and CR rate was 55% on both arms. DOCR was 38.6 months for quizartinib and 12.4 months for placebo.
QuANTUM-First was not designed to determine the benefit of quizartinib maintenance therapy. Of the subpopulation of patients who received maintenance, the HR for OS was 0.68; however, HR 1.62 in patients who received maintenance post-HSCT. Patients who received maintenance post-consolidation chemotherapy had HR for OS 0.40.
QuANTUM-R was an open-label, randomized (2:1) trial that randomized 245 patients with R/R FLT3-ITD positive AML to quizartinib monotherapy and 122 patients to salvage chemotherapy. Median OS was statistically greater on the quizartinib arm (HR 0.77; 95% CI: 0.59 – 0.99; p-value = 0.019). However, results of the OS analysis were not robust. CR+CRh rate for quizartinib was 11% and TI as achieved by 26% of patients for at least 56 days.
Quizartinib is effective for the treatment of patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML when combined with induction and consolidation chemotherapy followed by maintenance therapy. However, the efficacy of quizartinib maintenance post-HSCT was not established.
The OS results for quizartinib monotherapy versus salvage chemotherapy in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD positive AML are uncertain. However, the low CR+CRh rate of 11% and 26% TI rate indicate some monotherapy activity of quizartinib in patients with FLT3-ITD positive AML.
L96: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and Risk Management	Common adverse reactions (> 30%) not related to a laboratory evaluation for quizartinib + chemotherapy were febrile neutropenia, diarrhea, mucositis, and nausea.
Frequent serious adverse reactions (> 5%) for quizartinib + chemotherapy included febrile neutropenia.
QT interval prolongation, torsades de pointes, and cardiac arrest that were life-threatening or fatal occurred. Frequent ECG monitoring and electrolyte repletion are needed to prevent treatment-related mortality.
Common adverse reactions in the maintenance phase included cytopenias, gastrointestinal toxicities, and infections. Patients who received maintenance post-HSCT had a higher rate of adverse reactions to quizartinib.
The optimal dosage of quizartinib monotherapy during maintenance has not been established.
The arrhythmia risks of quizartinib during heart rate increases has not been established.
The protocol included monitoring for risks and instructions for intervention. With this is place, serious arrhythmias could be avoided.
The overall safety profile for quizartinib in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and cytarabine consolidation, and as maintenance monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy is acceptable for patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML, in light of the demonstrated survival benefit. However, the safety risks are not justified for maintenance therapy post-HSCT.
Safety information for quizartinib monotherapy at different dose levels during maintenance therapy, information on the arrhythmia risk with heart rate increases, and better characterization of the arrhythmia risk in the post-marketing setting is needed. A patient medication guide is required to inform and educate patients of the risk of arrhythmias and when to seek immediate medical attention.
Labeling should include a boxed warning for the serious risk of arrhythmias, instructions for monitoring, and dose modifications for toxicities.
A REMS with ETASU will be needed to inform prescribers of the unique risks of QTc interval prolongation, including risk factors (e.g.,
L97: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
hypokalemia, concomitant QTc prolonging medications), and mitigation strategies (e.g., strict QTc interval and electrolyte monitoring, dose modifications).

L105: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L106: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L107: ## The Applicant's Position:
L108: Acute myeloid leukemia (AML) is the most common acute leukemia in adults (Siegel, 2022; De Kouchkovsky, 2016). The United States (US) Surveillance, Epidemiology and End Results (SEER) Program of the National Cancer Institute (NCI) estimated that 20,240 people in the US would be diagnosed with AML in the year 2021, and 11,400 of these patients would die from the disease (accounting for 1.9% of all cancer deaths; SEER, 2021).
L109: The incidence of AML increases with age, ranging from 1.8 cases per 100,000 people aged &lt;65 years to 17.6 cases per 100,000 people aged &gt;65 years. More than half of the patients with newly diagnosed AML in developed countries are over 65 years of age, with a median age at diagnosis of 67 years (Heuser, 2020).
L110: Overall, the outcome of patients with AML is poor, with a 5-year survival rate of 40%, which rapidly declines with increasing age at diagnosis (Thein, 2013). Outcome is influenced by multiple factors, both disease-specific factors (cytogenetic and/or molecular genetic alterations, including FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 [*FLT3*], nucleophosmin 1 [*NPM1*], and others) and patient-specific factors, such as age, performance status (PS), organ function, and other comorbidities (Döhner, 2017). Age is the most prominent patient-specific risk factor, while chromosomal aberrations/genetic mutations have been considered the strongest disease-specific risk factors (Kottaridis, 2001). The assessment of genetic mutations, including *FLT3*, as prognostic factors has become increasingly important for risk assessment and in the treatment of AML as recommended by both the European LeukemiaNet (ELN) and the National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN) guidelines (Döhner, 2017; Pollyea, 2021).
L111: Overexpression of the *FLT3* receptor occurs in nearly all cases of AML, and mutations in *FLT3* represent one of the most common genetic alterations occurring in approximately 30% of adult patients with newly diagnosed AML (Papaemmanuil, 2016; Kennedy, 2020).
L112: There are 2 types of *FLT3* mutations: internal tandem duplication (ITD) mutations in the juxta-membrane domain (*FLT3*-ITD) and point mutations or deletion in the tyrosine kinase domain (*FLT3*-TKD). The *FLT3*-ITD mutation is more common than the TKD mutation, and these are found in 20% to 25% and 7% to 10% of all AML cases, respectively. Two methods are used to define *FLT3*-ITD positivity: the allelic ratio (AR), which calculates the ratio of the area under the plasma concentration-time curve (AUC) of mutant to wild-type (WT) alleles (*FLT3*-ITD/*FLT3*-WT), and variant allele frequency (VAF), which is defined as the number of mutant alleles divided by the number of total alleles, reported as a percentage (*FLT3*-
L113: ITD/*FLT3*-WT + *FLT3*-ITD). Patients harboring a *FLT3*-ITD mutation, particularly those with a high AR defined as a *FLT3*-ITD to *FLT3*-WT ratio of \ge 0.5, show increased relapse rates and inferior survival (Papaemmanuil, 2016; Patel, 2012; Thiede, 2002; Kottaridis, 2001). *FLT3*-ITD mutations are associated with a higher leukemic burden with marked leukocytosis and higher blast percentage. Females are affected more frequently and there is evidence that the incidence of *FLT3*-ITD mutations decreases with age, with an incidence of up to 35% in patients between 20 and 59 years compared with 16% to 20% in patients older than 60 years (Konig, 2015). In contrast, *FLT3*-TKD mutations have not been associated with a consistent prognostic impact (Mead, 2007).
L114: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L115: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment that AML is a serious disease that still presents therapeutic challenges since 5-year survival remains poor. FDA also agrees that *FLT3*-ITD is one of the most common genetic subtypes of AML.
L116: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options for Newly Diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML
L117: For subjects who are eligible to receive chemotherapy, standard chemotherapy is the mainstay of treatment for newly diagnosed AML, and cytarabine in combination with an anthracycline remains the standard induction therapy, regardless of cytogenetic or molecular abnormalities, with complete remission (CR) achieved in 60% to 85% of adults \le 60 years of age and 40% to 60% in adults &gt; 60 years of age (Döhner, 2015). For subjects who achieve a CR, the current guidelines recommend that patients must be offered post-induction therapy to eradicate residual disease and prevent relapse. Available options for consolidation include chemotherapy and/or allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (allo-HSCT) depending on the risk group. Allo-HSCT has improved the prognosis of patients with AML, but relapse remains high and non-relapse mortality associated with allo-HSCT is as high as 20% (Styczyński, 2020). Overall, cure rates following chemotherapy with or without HSCT are only 35% to 40% in patients under age 60 and 5% to 15% in patients over age 60 (Döhner, 2015).
L118: Per NCCN and ELN guidelines (Polleyea, 2021; Döhner, 2017), for AML patients with *FLT3* mutations (ITD or TKD) &lt; 60 years of age, midostaurin should be added to standard chemotherapy as part of frontline treatment. This recommendation is based on the results of the RATIFY study in newly diagnosed AML subjects with *FLT3* mutations (ITD and/or TKD) &lt; 60 years of age (Stone, 2017). For AML patients with *FLT3* mutations \ge 60 years benefiting from intensive cytarabine-based induction therapy, the NCCN recommends also that midostaurin be added to standard chemotherapy (Polleyea, 2021). For those who are not candidates for intensive chemotherapy, low-intensive regimens including venetoclax with hypomethylating agents such as azacitidine or decitabine are recommended (Polleyea, 2021;
L119: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L120: Döhner, 2017).
L121: Further, the safety and efficacy of midostaurin were evaluated in a single-arm Phase 2 study, AMLSG 16-10, in which midostaurin was added to intensive chemotherapy followed by allo-HSCT and single-agent midostaurin maintenance therapy in adult patients with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML (Schlenk, 2019). In this study, the authors reported that while older patients (61 to 70 years old) may benefit from midostaurin, outcomes were inferior to those observed in younger patients (18 to 60 years old) in terms of total CR rate (including CR with incomplete neutrophil or platelet recovery [CRi]), event-free survival (EFS), and 2-year OS. Less than 50% of patients finished the 1-year intended maintenance therapy, and the median time of maintenance therapy was 9 months after allo-HSCT and 10.5 months after high-dose cytarabine (HDAC) with premature termination mainly a result of causes other than relapse (gastrointestinal toxicity and infections).

L128: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L129: In the newly diagnosed *FLT3*-mutated AML treatment setting, midostaurin, a multitargeted kinase inhibitor, is the only drug that has been approved thus far based on the RATIFY study results. Given the poor prognosis of patients with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML and the high risk of relapse, the unmet medical need remains high and new treatment options are urgently needed.
L130: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L131: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment that midostaurin added to induction and consolidation chemotherapy is the only treatment specifically approved for newly diagnosed *FLT3*-mutated AML. We also agree that despite improvement in overall survival seen with the addition of midostaurin to induction and consolidation chemotherapy, that long-term overall survival remains guarded and represents continued unmet medical need.

The application was the subject of an Oncologic Drug Advisory Committee (ODAC) meeting in May 2019, and the FDA subsequently issued a Complete Response Letter in June 2019.

L148: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L149: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L150: The Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI) conducted inspections for QuANTUM-First at a clinical site in Valencia, Spain (Site 3402). This site was selected given high subject enrollment with remarkable OS results. Inspection review of the site led to a classification of No Action Indicated. The Applicant (Daiichi Sankyo, Inc.) was also audited. The classification for the Applicant inspection was also No Action Indicated. Based on these inspection results, the study data derived from the inspected clinical site was considered reliable in support of the requested indication.
L151: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L152: Quizartinib drug product (Vanflyta) is presented as round, film-coated tablets for oral use containing 17.7 or 26.5 mg quizartinib. The capsules are debossed with “DSC511” and “DSC512,” respectively. Inactive ingredients include hydroxypropyl betadex, microcrystalline cellulose, and magnesium stearate. The tablet coating contains hypromellose, talc, triacetin and titanium dioxide. All excipients, except for (b) (4) are comprised of compendial components, but the (b) (4) are comprised of compendial components. The CMC reviewer noted that the quantity of each excipient used in the manufacture of quizartinib tablets is below the “maximum potency” for oral administration as reported in the FDA Inactive Ingredient Database. Individual unspecified impurities are proposed to be controlled at a limit consistent with ICH Q3B (R2). The drug product is supplied in bottles of 14 or 28 tablets with an expiry of 60 months when stored at USP controlled room temperature.
L153: There were no outstanding safety issues identified for the manufacturing process or from the facilities inspections. The Applicant claimed a categorical exclusion from the requirement for an environmental assessment and the claim was accepted. Approval of the NDA was recommended by the Product Quality review team.
L154: Lastly, there are no impurity issues from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective. The proposed specifications for impurities in the drug substance and drug product that exceed the ICH qualification thresholds are justified. The Applicant conducted a 28-day impurity qualification toxicology study in rats to justify the higher limits.

L157: ## 4.4. Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
L158: The Applicant is seeking an indication for patients with newly-diagnosed AML limited to those who have a FLT3-ITD mutation, which is the target of quizartinib. In Study QuANTUM-First, patients were selected based on detection of a FLT3-ITD mutation with a clinical trial assay, and the results were confirmed retrospectively using the companion diagnostic LeukoStrat® CDx FLT3 Mutation Assay. It was determined that a device to select patients for therapy would be required for safe use of quizartinib when marketed. The Applicant cross-referenced PMA P160040 for the LeukoStrat® CDx FLT3 Mutation Assay, which identifies the FLT3-ITD mutation. The Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) reviewed the LeukoStrat® CDx FLT3 Mutation Assay for its intended use as a companion diagnostic to identify AML patients with a FLT3-ITD mutation for treatment with quizartinib under P160040/S011 (MDUFA date 4/12/2023). Following review, CDRH decided that the information provided was acceptable and the sPMA is approvable pending approval of quizartinib.
L159: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L160: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L161: Quizartinib (AC220) is a receptor tyrosine kinase (RTK) inhibitor that binds to Fms Related Receptor Tyrosine Kinase 3 (FLT3) and several other RTKs. Kinase inhibition is mediated through competition with the adenosine triphosphate (ATP) binding pocket of the kinase. Quizartinib binding is relatively specific for FLT3 (K_d = 1.3 nM) and KIT (K_d = 4.9 nM), as is the active metabolite AC886, with K_d values of 0.54 nM for FLT3 and 0.97 nM for KIT. In binding assays, quizartinib and AC886 showed approximately 10-fold lower affinity towards the FLT3 internal tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) mutation (K_d= 9.4 nM and 5.8 nM for quizartinib and AC886, respectively) compared to FLT3 (K_d= 1.3 nM and 0.54 nM for quizartinib and AC886, respectively).
L162: Quizartinib pharmacology was characterized in vitro and/or in vivo in MV4-11 cells and RS4;11 cells. MV4-11 cells are a human leukemia cell line that expresses FLT3 with an internal tandem duplication (ITD) activating mutation. The RS4;11 cell line is a leukemia cell line that expresses wild-type FLT3 and is not dependent on FLT3 activity for proliferation. Western blot analysis showed quizartinib induces concentration-dependent inhibition of autophosphorylation of FLT protein in MV4-11 cells in vitro. Quizartinib inhibited proliferation of cultured MV4-11 cells with an IC_{50} of 0.3 nM, whereas it had more than 1000-fold weaker activity against the RS4;11 control cell line (IC_{50} = 990 nM). Using the MV4-11 cell line, quizartinib also displayed in vivo anti-tumor activity in a mouse tumor xenograft model and increased the lifespan of animals.
L163: Quizartinib was tested for inhibitory activity against a panel of 118 non-kinase enzymes, receptors, channels, and transporters. Quizartinib produced greater than 50% inhibition in five of the 118 targets, but was more than 1000-fold less potent against those targets than for FLT3.
L164: Standalone in vitro and in vivo safety pharmacology studies were conducted to assess the cardiovascular risks of quizartinib and AC886, whereas endpoints to assess the effects of quizartinib on the central nervous and respiratory systems were incorporated into repeat-dose general toxicology studies. To assess the cardiovascular risks, in vitro studies examined the effects of quizartinib and its active metabolite on cloned human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) potassium, L-type calcium, hKvLQT1/mink potassium, and early and late sodium channels. Additionally, ex vivo studies were conducted using isolated rabbit hearts and ventricular tissues and in dispersed canine cardiomyocytes, while in vivo cardiovascular data was collected in the repeat-dose toxicology studies in dogs and monkeys, as well as in a
L165: dedicated study in telemetered monkeys. Overall, these studies demonstrated quizartinib exposure was associated with inhibition of potassium channel currents, prolongation of QT intervals, and transient increases in blood pressure.
L166: The pharmacokinetics of quizartinib were characterized in the mouse, rat, dog, and monkey. Following oral administration of quizartinib, the plasma exposures were approximately dose proportional in the range of doses tested in nonclinical species. Female exposures were 1.4- to 2-fold higher than males in rats and dogs. Bioavailability was lower in monkeys than in rats and dogs (15% versus 30% to 40%). Both quizartinib and AC886 are highly bound to plasma proteins (\ge 99\%) in animals and humans. Quizartinib is well distributed in all species. Following oral administration of radioactive quizartinib in rats, the highest levels of radioactivity observed at Tmax were found in the GI tract, pancreas, kidney, liver, lymphoid tissues (spleen, thymus, and lymph nodes), adrenal gland, pituitary, thyroid gland, adipose tissues, myocardium, lung, bone marrow, and male and female reproductive organs (such as prostate and ovary). The lowest levels were in the central nervous system and bone. In animals, the excretion was primarily hepatobiliary, with low levels excreted in the urine.
L167: Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted in Sprague-Dawley rats, beagle dogs, and cynomolgus monkeys given quizartinib dihydrochloride for up to 13 weeks. All dose levels employed were adequately justified. Hematologic and lymphoid toxicities were common and attributable to exaggerated pharmacology. Target organs of toxicity included the bone marrow (rats, dogs, and monkeys), liver (dogs and monkeys), kidney (rats and dogs), thymus (rats, dogs, and monkeys), ovary, vagina, and testes (rats and monkeys). Toxicologically significant microscopic findings included bone marrow hypopcellularity, extramedullary hematopoiesis, liver crystals, single cell necrosis of the renal tubule and renal tubular basophilia and crystals, and thymic lymphoid atrophy/necrosis. When quizartinib was administered to juvenile rats, the toxicities were similar to those in adult rats. A 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study conducted in rats supports the proposed related substance impurity specifications, which are higher than ICH qualification thresholds.
L168: In an embryo-fetal development (EFD) study in rats, quizartinib at doses resulting in exposures approximately 3 times the exposure at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) was associated with adverse developmental outcomes including structural abnormalities (anasarca and edema) and alterations to growth (lower fetal weights and effects on skeletal ossification). The embryofetal toxicities occurred in the absence of maternal toxicities. Because of the potential for serious adverse reactions in a breastfed child, patients will be advised that breastfeeding is not recommended during treatment and for 1 month after the last dose; the recommendation takes into consideration the elimination half-life of quizartinib and the active metabolite, AC886. Based on the EFD study and genotoxicity findings, female patients of reproductive potential will be advised to use effective contraception during treatment and for
L169: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L170: at least 7 months after the last dose, and male patients with female partners of reproductive potential will be advised to use effective contraception during treatment and for at least 4 months after the last dose.
L171: In genotoxicity studies, quizartinib was mutagenic in a bacterial reverse mutation assay. Quizartinib was not genotoxic in the following assays: mouse lymphoma thymidine kinase mutation assay, chromosome aberration assay in human peripheral blood lymphocytes, and rat bone marrow micronucleus assay (tested to the maximum tolerated dose, MTD). Of note, an equivocal result (positive genotoxicity but within the range of historical control data) at a dose approximating the MTD was obtained in the micronucleus assessment in a 28-day rat toxicology study. An additional genotoxicity study was conducted in transgenic F344 homozygous Big Blue rats, and the results were negative. No carcinogenicity studies were conducted or are required to support the marketing of quizartinib for the current indication.
L172: Fertility studies in animals have not been conducted with quizartinib; however, adverse findings in male and female reproductive systems were observed in repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and monkeys. In female rats, ovarian cysts and vaginal mucification modifications were observed at an exposure comparable to the MRHD based on area under the curve (AUC). Findings in female monkeys included atrophy of the uterus, ovary, and vagina at approximately 0.2 times the MRHD. In male rats, testicular seminiferous tubular degeneration and failure of sperm release were observed at approximately 6 times the MRHD. Findings in male monkeys included germ cell depletion in the testes at approximately 0.4 times the MRHD. Findings were reversible after a recovery period of about one month, except the vaginal mucosal modifications in the female rats.
L173: The nonclinical pharmacology and toxicology data submitted to this NDA are adequate to support the approval of quizartinib for the proposed indication.

L179: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L180: ## Primary pharmacology
L181: **Table 3 – In Vitro Pharmacology of Quizartinib**

Competition binding assay of quizartinib	T7 phage strains prepared in an E. coli host (BL21 strain) or in HEK-293 cells for 441 kinases	In vitro	Quizartinib binds with the highest affinity to FLT3 (K_d = 1.3 \text{ nM}).

L183: Source: eCTD M4.2.1.1 Studies NR0006, NR0007, NR0118, and NR0119
L184: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L185: FDA acknowledges the tabulated information above. The following provides a more detailed review of the in vitro and in vivo studies.
L186: Research has identified molecular targets for treating AML. Among molecular abnormalities, FLT3 mutations are frequent and well characterized actionable mutations in AML. FLT3 encodes a receptor tyrosine kinase expressed on normal hematopoietic stem/progenitor cells. FLT3 dimerizes and autophosphorylates upon the binding of the FLT3 ligand (FLT3L), activating the intracellular tyrosine kinase domain (TKD), which causes phosphorylation of downstream molecules and activation of signaling cascades that promote the transcription of genes
L187: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L188: regulating survival, proliferation and differentiation. Mutations of FLT3 resulting in constitutive signaling are common in AML, including internal tandem duplications in the juxtamembrane domain (FLT3-ITD) and point mutations. Patients with AML with FLT3-ITDs may have a poor prognosis.
L189: Using an array of in vitro and in vivo studies, the binding affinity and kinetics of quizartinib and its metabolite AC886 have been characterized, as well as the inhibition of FLT3 activity and anti-proliferative effects in leukemia cells.
L190: ## In Vitro Studies
L191: ## Binding assay (cell-free studies)
L192: The binding affinity and kinetics of quizartinib and AC886 were evaluated for RTKs. KinomeScan technology was used to determine the biochemical potency and selectivity of quizartinib and AC886 against panels of 441 and 391 kinases, respectively (Study #NR0119 and #NR0118). The assay measured the ability of the test article to compete with the interaction between the target kinase and a known ATP site binding ligand. Quizartinib and AC886 displayed high binding affinity to RTKs (with K_d values &lt;100 nM) with similar characteristics and bound to a few closely related RTKs with comparable K_d values.
L193: Quizartinib had lower affinity towards the FLT3 internal tandem duplication, FLT3-ITD (K_d= 9.4 nM), compared to FLT3 (K_d= 1.3 nM). This trend was also noted for the AC886 metabolite (K_d= 5.8 nM for FLT3-ITD and 0.54 nM for FLT3).

L199: The total (bound + unbound) C_{max} values of quizartinib and AC886 are 0.94 \muM and 0.45 \muM, respectively, in subjects taking an oral dose of 53 mg quizartinib (free base; Study No. 2689-CL-2004).
L200: ## Intracellular inhibition of FLT3 activity
L201: The MV4-11 cell line is derived from human leukemia that expresses the FLT3-ITD activating mutation that results in constitutive activation of FLT3 (Yee et al., Blood, 100: 2941-2949, 2002). Quizartinib appears to bind within the catalytic domain of the FLT3 protein, competing with ATP, thereby inhibiting the ability of the enzyme to phosphorylate proteins. Quizartinib induced concentration-dependent inhibition of FLT protein autophosphorylation (Study #NR0006).
L202: The effect of quizartinib on the proliferation of MV4-11 cells was also evaluated. In study #NR0007, the RS4;11 cell line served as the negative control. The RS4;11 cell line expresses wild-type FLT3 and is not dependent on FLT3 activity for proliferation. The leukemia cells were
L203: incubated with quizartinib (0.169 nM to 3 µM) for 72 h (MV4-11 cells) or 48 h (RS4;11 cells). Quizartinib inhibited FLT3-dependent cell proliferation in the MV4-11 cell line (IC_{50} = 0.3 nM), while it exerted 1000-fold weaker activity against FLT3-independent proliferation of the RS4;11 cells (IC_{50} = 990 nM).
L204: ## In Vivo Studies
L205: ## Pharmacodynamic analysis: temporal effect of FLT3 inhibition
L206: The inhibitory effect of quizartinib on the phosphorylation of FLT3 was assessed in vivo (Study #NR0112). MV4-11 tumor bearing female C.B-17-SCID mice were treated with a single oral dose of either vehicle ((b)4% 2-hydroxypropyl-\beta-cyclodextrin [HBCD]) or quizartinib (10 mg/kg, n = 4 mice per timepoint) and tumors were collected at 0.25, 1, 2, 6, 16, 24, 48, and 96 hours after dosing. The tumors were harvested for FLT3 analysis, and plasma samples were collected for pharmacokinetic (PK) analysis of quizartinib and AC886. Phosphorylated FLT3 (p-FLT3) and total FLT3 protein were measured in tumor lysates with Meso Scale Discovery (MSD) analysis. A normalized percent p-FLT3 to total FLT3 ratio was calculated using the values for the vehicle at the 0.25-hour timepoint.
L207: A time-dependent reduction of p-FLT3 was observed following a single dose of 10 mg/kg quizartinib. The maximal effect was observed at 1-, 2-, and 6-hours post-dose with a percent p-FLT3 value of 6%, 4%, and 7%, respectively. The p-FLT3 leveled off, i.e., ~60%, at 48-96 hours post-dose for quizartinib treatment, while vehicle treated animals remained stable over time and p-FLT3 ranged from 70%-126%. The results demonstrated that quizartinib treatment induced a temporal effect on the inhibition of auto-phosphorylation of FLT3. Exposure levels of both quizartinib and AC886 in the tumor and plasma samples over 48 h were comparable.
L208: ## Anti-tumor activity in xenograft models
L209: MV4-11 cells were implanted subcutaneously (SC) in mice to form localized tumors or injected intravenously (IV) to produce disseminated disease. In the latter case (Study #NR0116), the leukemia cells infiltrated the bone marrow. Quizartinib (0.1 to 10 mg/kg) was administered orally in mice after disease was established. In the SC tumor model (Study #NR0009), the anti-tumor activity of quizartinib treatment was both dose-dependent and treatment duration-dependent. In the disseminated graft model (Study #NR0116), quizartinib treatment prolonged the lifespan of animals in a dose-dependent fashion. Treatment with 1 mg/kg quizartinib once daily for 31 days produced an increased lifespan of 55%.

L244: Type of Study	Major Findings
Absorption
Pharmacokinetics and Dose Proportionality of Quizartinib After a Single Dose in Rats (Study NR0016)	In male rats, quizartinib levels were approximately proportional to dose. Quizartinib has good bioavailability (F) in male and female rats (36% to 46% in male and 41% to 47% in female rats over dose range of 3 to 30 mg/kg). In fed rats (male) the bioavailability increased to approximately 100%, indicating that the presence of food enhanced the absorption of quizartinib.
Pharmacokinetics and Dose Proportionality of Quizartinib After a Single Dose in Dogs (Study NR0014)	In male dogs, PK characteristics after oral dosing appeared to be relatively dose proportional over the dose range of 1 to 10 mg/kg. Oral bioavailability was good at approximately 30% to 40%.

Metabolism
Metabolite Profiling of [14C]Quizartinib in Rats (Study 021390-1)	Metabolite profiling in plasma and feces from rats given [14C]quizartinib dihydrochloride revealed that AC886 was the major metabolite in rat plasma. No other circulating metabolite was present at a fraction greater than 6.1% at any time point (2, 4, or 24 h).
Metabolite Profiling of [14C]Quizartinib in Dogs (Study 021391-1)	AC886 was the major circulating metabolite at later time points, but another major metabolite at a fraction greater than 15%, identified as a morpholino oxidation product, was also found. This metabolite was not detected as a major plasma component in rats or humans, indicating species differences in oxidation of the morpholino side chain. No other circulating metabolite was present at a fraction greater than 8.2% at any time point (2, 4, or 24 h).

TK data from general toxicology studies
4-week and 13-week repeat-dose toxicology studies in: rats (Study 70040 and 802411); dogs (Study 60024 and 802412); monkeys (Study 1008-2493 and 803726)
In rats, plasma exposure (AUC) to quizartinib increased greater than dose increment with oral doses up to 60 mg/kg/day. After 3 months repeated dosing, exposures were greater than on day 1 by approximately 3-fold. No apparent sex difference was observed.
In dogs, Cmax and AUC increased greater than dose increment at doses up to 150 mg/kg/day after 4 weeks, and almost with dose increment up to 15 mg/kg/day after 3 months. After 3 months repeated dosing, there were decreases in Cmax at all doses up to 15 mg/kg/day and decreases in AUC at 15 mg/kg/day only. Gender effects were not observed after 4 weeks of dosing, however, at 15 mg/kg/day over 3 months, Cmax and AUC were lower in males than females.
In monkeys, systemic exposure to quizartinib was generally with dose increment up to 3 months dosing and at doses up to 60 mg/kg/day. Plasma exposures for quizartinib and the major metabolite, AC886, following repeated quizartinib dosing were higher than Day 1 exposures. Quizartinib and AC886 displayed no consistent gender effects in monkeys.
TK data from reproductive toxicology studies
Definitive oral (gavage) study for effects on embryo-fetal development in the rat (Study 901985)
Exposure to quizartinib was greater than dose increment, whereas exposure to AC886 increased with dose increment. There was no relevant accumulation of either analyte after 12 days of administration, except for a slight increase in exposure of quizartinib at 6 mg/kg/day. AUC0-1 values of AC886 relative to quizartinib were 42% to 83% on Gestation Day (GD) 6 and 32% to 75% on GD 17
L249: from 0.6 to 6 mg/kg/day. Similarly, Cmax values of AC886 relative to quizartinib were 41% to 78% on Gestation Day (GD) 6 and 31% to 77% on GD 17 from 0.6 to 6 mg/kg/day.

L254: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L255: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L256: Single-dose and repeated-dose toxicity studies for up to 13 weeks of daily quizartinib administration have been conducted in rats, dogs, and monkeys. At the early phase of development, the dog was used to characterize the toxicity of quizartinib as a nonrodent species. However, the dog showed a unique major circulating metabolite (morpholino oxidation product) that has not been detected in human, rat, and monkey, despite achieving higher exposure of those species than the dog to quizartinib, and may possibly contribute to a dog-specific liver toxicity due to birefringent crystal deposition. Based on pharmacokinetic (PK) and toxicology data, the cynomolgus monkey was selected as a potential alternate nonrodent model for evaluating the toxicity profile of quizartinib to predict human safety risk.
L257: In all animal species studied, the principal target organs of toxicity were the bone marrow and lymphoid organs. In addition, toxicological effects were observed in the kidney, liver, ovary, vagina, and testis. Target-organ toxicity appeared to be dose and time dependent, and most toxicological findings were reversible following a 28-day or 30-day recovery period; however, slight decreases in red blood cell (RBC) and white blood cell (WBC) parameters, minimal bone marrow hypocellularity, and liver crystal deposition (only in dogs) persisted through the post recovery period. In 13-week studies, toxicological findings were present among rats and dogs at 10 mg/kg/day and 15 mg/kg/day, respectively, and these findings included decreases in hematology parameters, increased liver enzymes, and microscopic changes in bone marrow and lymphoid organs. In a 13-week study in monkeys, mortality and adverse clinical signs were reported at 10/6 mg/kg/day, with the findings consistent with those reported in the other species. NOAELs in the 13-week repeated dose toxicity studies were 3 mg/kg/day in rats, 5 mg/kg/day in dogs, and 3 mg/kg/day in monkeys.
L258: ## Rat 28-day Repeat-dose Toxicity Study
L259: Report Title: AC010220: A 28-Day Oral Toxicity Study in Sprague-Dawley Rats
Study Number: 70040 (eCTD 4.2.3.2)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:All animals in the 60/30 mg/kg/day dose group were found dead or prematurely euthanized from Study Days 3 to 15. Cause of death/morbidity was considered to be due to multi-organ toxicity related to deteriorated condition. Gross and microscopic histopathologic changes were noted in the kidneys, bone marrow, thymus, spleen, mandibular and mesenteric lymph nodes, liver, adrenals, testes, epididymides, heart, pancreas, stomach, and small and large intestines (data tables not shown).
Once daily dosing at 5 and 15 mg/kg/day for 28 days was well tolerated with target organ toxicities noted in bone marrow, kidney and thymus.

L372: ## Toxicokinetics
L373: Cmax and AUC0-24 of quizartinib and AC886 increased as a function of quizartinib dose in both sexes. The plasma exposures for both analytes following repeated quizartinib dosing were higher than Day 1 exposures. Quizartinib and AC886 displayed no consistent gender effects in monkeys.

L375: ## Observations and Results: Changes From Control
L376: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	5 animals found dead or prematurely euthanized between Days 6 and 36, including 2F/1M at 30/12 mg/kg/day and 2F at 10/6 mg/kg/day. Two deaths were due to anemia, one was likely secondary to marked bone marrow hypocellularity. A cause of death could not be determined for the others.
Clinical Signs	\ge 6 mg/kg/day: reduced appetite, emesis, prominent backbone, decreased activity
\ge 10 mg/kg/day: severe dehydration, hunched posture, decreased respiratory rate, labored breathing, partly closed eyes, severe dehydration, soft/liquid feces, severe emesis
\ge 12 mg/kg/day: moderate to severe lack of coordination, severe dehydration; dosing discontinued on Study Days 61 (M) and 60 (F) and all animals prematurely euthanized
\ge 30 mg/kg/day: dosing suspended for 10 days due to moribundity/premature death in 1M/2F and severity of clinical signs in all animals. Dosing resumed at a reduced dose of 12 mg/kg/day until dosing discontinued as noted above.
Body Weights	\ge 6 mg/kg/day: decreased body weight
Ophthalmoscopy	No treatment-related changes
Electrocardiography	No treatment-related effects
Hematology	\ge 3 mg/kg/day: decreases in RBC, hemoglobin, hematocrit
10/6 mg/kg/day: decreases in reticulocytes; increases in platelets, MCV, RDW
30/12 mg/kg/day: increases in neutrophils; decreases in lymphocytes and eosinophils
Clinical Chemistry	3 mg/kg/day: decreases in total protein and globulin (M only)
10/6 mg/kg/day: increases in AST, ALT, BUN (M only); decreases in total protein, albumin, globulin and phosphorus
30/12 mg/kg/day: increases in AST, ALT, TBL (M only), BUN, creatinine, glucose (M only), triglycerides; decreases in total protein, albumin, globulin, phosphorus, sodium, potassium and chloride (M only)
Urinalysis	No treatment-related changes
Gross Pathology	10/6 mg/kg/day: small thymus (F only)
30/12 mg/kg/day: small thymus (M only); enlarged adrenals; small spleen in 1 female
Organ Weights	\ge 3 mg/kg/day: decreased uterus, ovaries, spleen
\ge 10/6 mg/kg/day: increased liver, adrenals; decreased thymus (F only)
30/12 mg/kg/day: decreased thymus

L390: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L391: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant. See additional information below from the 13-week studies in rats and monkeys related to findings in the reproductive organs to assist with information presented under section 13.1 of the product label.
L392: ## **A 90 Day Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of AC220 (with a 30 Day Recovery) in the Albino Rat (#802411; GLP)**
L393: - Doses were 1 (Low dose. LD), 3 (mid dose, MD), and 10 mg/kg (high dose, HD).
- Adverse reproductive effects in females included ovarian cyst, abnormal vaginal epithelia and mucification. Those in males included small testis and epididymis, oligo/aspermia, and failure of sperm release. Findings were reversible.
- The findings in females started at the mid dose (AUC of 12,500 ng•h/mL) and the findings in males occurred at the high dose (10 mg/kg; AUC of 65,400 ng•h/mL).
- Based on the human AUC of quizartinib (10,200 ng•h/mL at the MRHD), the adverse reproductive effects in female rats occurred at an exposure comparable to human exposure. That in male rats occurred at ~6-fold the human exposure.

HD (females)

L397: ## **A 13 Week Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of AC220 in the Cynomolgus Monkey with a 4 Week Recovery Period (#803726; GLP)**
L398: - Doses were 3 (LD), 10/6 (MD), and 30/12 mg/kg (HD).
- Histopathology findings were seen in male (oligo/aspermia, germ cell depletion in testes) and female (uterine, ovarian, and vaginal atrophy) reproductive organs. Findings were reversible.
- Effects occurred starting at the MD in males (AUC of 4,060 ng•h/mL for quizartinib and on D91) and females (AUC of 2,440 ng•h/mL for quizartinib on D91).
- Based on the human AUC of quizartinib (10,200 ng•h/mL at the MRHD), adverse reproductive findings in animals occurred at 0.2 (females)- to 0.4 (males)- fold human exposures.

L410: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L411: ## The Applicant's Position:
L412: Quizartinib demonstrated potential for mutagenicity in a bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames test), but not in a mammalian cell mutation assay (mouse lymphoma thymidine kinase) or transgenic rodent gene mutation assay with Big Blue® rats. Quizartinib was negative in a chromosome aberration assay and in a single-dose rat bone marrow micronucleus assay although a rat micronucleus assay conducted in conjunction with the 28-day general toxicology study had an equivocal result.
L413: Genotoxicity Assessments
Report Title: AC010220 Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay	
Study Number: 961163 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.1)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
Following exposure to quizartinib, increases in revertant colony counts with TA98 in the absence and presence of S9 mix, and TA100 in the presence of S9 mix.
Exposure to quizartinib in the presence of S9 mix caused partial absence of the background lawn of non-revertant bacteria indicating that quizartinib was toxic to the bacteria at high-dose concentrations.

Test system: S. typhimurium (TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537) and E. coli (WP2uvrA)
Test validity: Mean revertant colony counts for the vehicle controls were close to or within the laboratory historical control range. Positive controls induced increases in revertant colony counts.
Report Title: AC010220 Mammalian Cell Mutation Assay
Study Number: 962099 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.1)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
No substantial increases in mutant frequency were noted for quizartinib in the absence or presence of S9 mix at dose levels up to the limit of toxicity.
Quizartinib did not show any evidence of genotoxicity in this assay.

Test system: Mouse lymphoma L5178Y TK+/− (clone 3.7.2) cells
Test validity: Plating efficiencies for vehicle control cultures were within acceptable limits (65-120% for Day 2/viability). Positive controls caused increases in the small colony mutant frequency in each treatment regime.
L414: Genotoxicity Assessments
Report Title: AC010220 Chromosome Aberration Assay
Study Number: 961164 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.1)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
Cultures treated with quizartinib did not show any significant increases in the incidence of cells with aberrant metaphases at levels up to the limit of toxicity
A substantial reduction in the absolute number of analyzable metaphases was observed at the high dose for the 4-hour treatments.

Test system: Human peripheral blood lymphocytes
Test validity: Positive controls caused significant increases in the proportion of cells with chromosome damage.
Report Title: AC010220 Bone Marrow Micronucleus Assay in Rats Following a Single Dose of Quizartinib
Study Number: 961901 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.2)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
At quizartinib doses up to 100 mg/kg, no significant increases in the incidence of micronucleated immature erythrocytes were noted.
Quizartinib showed a marginal but statistically significant decrease in the proportion of immature erythrocytes at the 48-hour sampling time for animals in the highest dose group.

Test system:
Rat (Hsd:SD): (Males - 7 weeks; Females - 8 weeks); Single oral dose administration at 15, 50 and 100 mg/kg; positive control (cyclophosphamide) at 20 mg/kg; cells evaluated from bone marrow smears included micronucleated immature and mature erythrocytes, and proportion of immature erythrocytes in the total population (immature and mature erythrocytes).
Test validity:
Data for concurrent vehicle control were within ranges determined from laboratory historical data. Positive control induced clear, unequivocal increases in micronuclei.
Report Title: AC010220: Micronucleus Test Component of the Rat 28-Day Oral Toxicity Study
Study Number: 961165 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.2)	GLP Compliance: Yes
L415: Genotoxicity Assessments
Key Study Findings:
At 15 mg/kg/day only, significant increases in the incidence of micronucleated immature erythrocytes were observed, although none of the individual values or group means for quizartinib fell outside the historical control range. As a result, it cannot be concluded whether the small apparent increase was treatment-related or due to variation between animals/groups. Therefore, the results of the study are considered equivocal.

Test system:
Rat (Crl:CD [SD]): (Males/Females; 9-10 weeks); oral daily dose administration at 5 and 15 mg/kg/day for 28 days; bone marrow samples were taken for bone marrow smears and 2000 immature erythrocytes per animal were examined for presence of micronuclei. The proportion of immature erythrocytes was also assessed for potential bone marrow toxicity. Positive control slides from 3 male and 3 female rats dosed orally with cyclophosphamide monohydrate at 20 mg/kg in a separate group were included.

Test validity:
Positive control slides showed significant increases in the incidence of micronucleated immature erythrocytes.

Report Title: Transgenic Rat Gene Mutation Assay with Quizartinib
Study Number: AF84WP.171.BTL (eCTD 4.2.3.3.2)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:All animals survived in the main study
Quizartinib did not cause elevated mutant frequencies at the cII gene in the liver, bone marrow or glandular stomach of treated animals and therefore was considered negative for induction of mutagenicity

Test system:
Rat (F344 homozygous Big Blue® transgenic): based on 7-day RF study in non-transgenic F344 rats at 15, 30 and 45 mg/kg/day which resulted in premature death at doses \ge30 mg/kg/day, 6 males/group (8-12 weeks) were administered quizartinib at 3.8, 7.5 and 15 mg/kg/day for 28 days daily dosing; positive control (N-ethyl-N-nitrosourea [ENU]) at 20 mg/kg/day was given for 6 non-consecutive days; bone marrow, liver and glandular stomach tissues were collected at necropsy and evaluated for mutation analysis via DNA isolation from the first 5 animals/group and analysis of cII mutants.

Test validity:
Vehicle control did not cause statistically elevated mutant frequency at the cII gene in the liver, bone marrow, or glandular stomach, and positive control induced a statistically significant increase in mutant frequency for all tissues tested.
L416: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L417: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's conclusions on the genotoxicity assessment of quizartinib. Quizartinib was positive in the Ames test and negative in both in vitro (mouse lymphoma thymidine kinase) and in vivo (transgenic rat model) mammalian cell mutation tests. FDA also agrees that quizartinib was not clastogenic and did not cause polyploidy in a chromosomal aberration assay (human peripheral blood lymphocytes). While quizartinib was negative in bone marrow micronucleus assays in a single dose study in rats, the in vivo micronucleus assay conducted in conjunction with the 28-day general toxicology study in rats was considered equivocal (positive results compared to concurrent control but within the historical control range).
L418: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L419: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L420: In accordance with International Conference on Harmonisation (ICH) S9, no studies have been conducted.
L421: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L422: FDA concurs with the Applicant's statement.
L423: ## 5.5.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L424: Embryo-fetal toxicity studies in rats showed no maternal toxicity and no evidence of quizartinib-related embryolethality up to 6 mg/kg/day. Fetotoxicity and teratogenicity were evident at 6 mg/kg/day, primarily by lower fetal weights, with effects on skeletal ossification, and fetal abnormalities (anasarca). The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for embryo-fetal development was 2 mg/kg/day.
L425: Report Title: An Oral Range-finding Teratology Study of AC220 in the Rat
Study Number: 901971 (eCTD 4.2.3.5.2)	GLP Compliance: No
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
Dams:
Treatment-related maternal toxicity included clinical signs (dehydration, skin pallor, thinness, swollen cranium, increased incidence of fur staining, and liquid vaginal discharge) at 20 mg/kg/day. One female at 10 mg/kg/day had brown liquid vaginal discharge and delivered dead fetal material on GD21.
Slightly lower body weight, body-weight gain and food consumption in dams at 10 mg/kg/day as well as reduced litter size and fetal body weights.
At 20 mg/kg/day embryo-fetal lethality as evidenced by total resorption noted in all animals and at 10 mg/kg/day in 1 of 5 animals.
At 10 mg/kg/day, a high number of late resorptions resulting in higher post-implantation loss occurred. Lower gravid uterus weight was observed due to smaller litters and lower fetal weights.

Fetus:
At 10 mg/kg/day, lower fetal weight were noted. In all live litters from this group, fetuses had anasarca. Some showed generalized skin pallor and micrognathia. One live fetus had microcaudia.

Methods
Species/Strain: Rat/Crl:CD(SD)	Route of administration: oral at doses of 1, 3, 10 and 20 mg/kg/day
Number/Sex/Group: 5 females	Method of administration: gavage
Initial Age: Dams 11 weeks	Vehicle/Formulation: (b) (4)% HP\betaCD/Suspension
L426: Report Title: An Oral Embryo-fetal Development Toxicity Study of AC220 in the Rat
Study Number: 901985 (eCTD 4.2.3.5.2)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
No treatment-related maternal toxicity was observed in dams.
The gravid uterus weights were slightly lower (-11% vs vehicle control) at 6 mg/kg/day and fetal weights were 12% to 13% less than controls at 6 mg/kg/day.
At 6 mg/kg/day, higher incidence of major malformations and minor external anomalies were noted. The most common abnormalities included anasarca, edema on the top of the hind paws and edema of the ventral cervical region.
Higher incidence of fetuses with incomplete ossification of the frontal bones and thoracic centrum variants were observed at 6 mg/kg/day.

Methods
Species/Strain: Rat/Crl:CD(SD)	Route of administration: oral at doses of 0.6, 2 and 6 mg/kg/day
Number/Sex/Group: 25 females	Method of administration: gavage
Satellite Group: 3 females

L427: ## Toxicokinetics
L428: Exposure to quizartinib and AC886 was increased with increasing dose increment. There was no notable accumulation of either analyte after 12 days of administration, apart from the slight increase in exposure at 6 mg/kg/day. Cmax and AUC0-t values of AC886 relative to quizartinib were 41% to 83% on GD 6 and 31% to 77% on GD 17 from 0.6 to 6 mg/kg/day.

L449: ## 5.5.5. Other Toxicology Studies
L450: ## The Applicant's Position:
L451: Quizartinib was not considered to be a contact sensitizer and was not phototoxic. An impurities study to assess potential toxicity of five impurities of quizartinib showed no adverse effects in a 28-day rat study.
L452: Antigenicity	
Report Title: A Sensitization Study of AC220 by Dermal Administration in Guinea Pigs-Modified Buehler Design	
Study Number: 20079155 (eCTD 4.2.3.7.1)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
No dermal reactions to 100% quizartinib in the test and challenge control animals occurred. Dermal scores were 0.
Following challenge with 2.5% w/v HCA, dermal scores of 1 or 2 were noted in 5/10 HCA test animals at the 24- and 48-hour scoring intervals. Dermal reactions in the remaining HCA test and HCA control animals were limited to scores of 0 or \pm. Group mean dermal scores were higher in the HCA test animals compared to the HCA challenge control animals.
Following challenge with 1.0% w/v HCA, dermal scores of 1 were noted in 2/10 HCA test animals at the 24- and 48-hour scoring intervals. Dermal reactions in the HCA challenge control animals were limited to scores of 0. Group mean dermal scores were higher in the HCA test animals compared to the HCA challenge control animals.
Based on these results, quizartinib is not considered to be a contact sensitizer in guinea pigs.

Species/Strain: Hartley-derived albino guinea pig	Route of administration: dermal
Number/Sex/Group: 10	Method of administration: topical
Initial Age: 6-7 Weeks	Vehicle/Formulation: Suspension - Reverse Osmosis Deionized (RODI) water
Positive control: \alpha-Hexylcinnamaldehyde
Method: Guinea pigs were topically treated with 100% quizartinib, 0.3 g/site, once per week, for 3 consecutive weeks. Following a 2-week rest period, a challenge was performed; 20 test and 10 previously untreated (naïve) challenge control guinea pigs were topically treated with 100% quizartinib. Challenge responses in the test animals were compared with those of the challenge control animals. An \alpha-Hexylcinnamaldehyde (HCA) positive control group consisting of 10 HCA test and 10 HCA control guinea pigs was included. The animals were treated as above with the HCA test animals receiving 5% w/v HCA in ethanol for induction and 2.5% and 1.0% w/v HCA in acetone for challenge.
L453: Impurity Qualification
Report Title: A 28-day oral gavage toxicity Study of AC220 Impurities in Rats
Study Number: 1258-023 (eCTD 4.2.3.7.6)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
Hematologic changes including reduced red cell parameters (RBC, hemoglobin and/or hematocrit) and reticulocytes, neutrophils and eosinophils were observed in all quizartinib-treatment groups, and correlated with decreased bone marrow cellularity. These changes were considered related to quizartinib and not the added impurities.
Hepatocellular effects including increased AST and ALT were noted at 5 mg/kg/day of quizartinib with (b)/(4)% of impurities. These increases were likely related to the impurities as similar increases were not observed in animals receiving quizartinib alone; however, these effects were not considered adverse due to the small magnitude of change and lack of microscopic correlates.
Statistically significant organ weight changes of the testes, spleen, and thymus were observed in males and/or females. Although these changes were seen only in groups given quizartinib with impurities, they were not considered adverse due to the small magnitude of change and lack of microscopic correlates.
In summary, no adverse effects were noted for any parameter examined for quizartinib impurities concentrations of (b)/(4)% or (b)/(4)% after oral gavage administration for 28 days.

Methods
Species/Strain: Rat/Crl:CD(SD)	Route of administration: oral
Number/Sex/Group: 10	Method of administration: gavage
Initial Age: 6 Weeks	Vehicle/Formulation: (b)/(4)% HP\betaCD/Suspension
Impurities included:
Impurity levels: (b)/(4) mg/kg/day, (b)/(4) mg/kg/day representing (b)/(4)% and (b)/(4)% of 5 mg/kg/day quizartinib dose, respectively.
Justification: In a 28-day rat toxicology study, quizartinib was administered at 5 and 15 mg/kg/day by oral gavage. The primary treatment-related findings included a dose-dependent decrease in RBC, hemoglobin, hematocrit, neutrophil, monocyte and eosinophil counts. Minimal bone marrow hypocellularity was observed at 5 mg/kg/day. Slight elevations in AST, ALT and ALP levels were noted only at 15 mg/kg/day. Thus, a level of 5 mg/kg/day represents a low dose where minimal drug-related changes were observed and measurable. The goal of this study was to evaluate the potential additional toxicity of two concentrations of impurities spiked into the parent compound quizartinib.

Key Study Findings:Since quizartinib only showed cytotoxicity in the presence of UVA, the exact PIF could not be calculated (mean PIF >1.576)
Mean MPE was <0.15
Results indicate no phototoxic potential of quizartinib based on ICH S10.

Test system: BALB/3T3 mouse fibroblasts (clone A31)
Method: Quizartinib dissolved in 1% DMSO/DPBS at concentrations up to 10.5 mg/L or the positive control article CPZ (range 0.1 to 120 mg/L) in mouse fibroblasts exposed to 5 J/cm2 of UVA and approximately 18 mJ/cm2 of UVB.
Test validity: Cytotoxic and photocytotoxic responses of the positive control were as anticipated.
Ocular Irritation Toxicity
Report Title: An Acute Irritation Study of AC220 by Ocular Administration in Rabbits
Study Number: 20079153 (eCTD 4.2.3.7.7)	GLP Compliance: Yes
Test system: Rabbit/NZW; 3 females administered a single ocular dose of 0.0354 g/eye with examination for irritation at 1, 24, 48, and 72 hours post administration
Key Study Findings:Exposure to quizartinib produced conjunctivitis in 3/3 test eyes at the 1-h scoring interval. Complete resolution of conjunctivitis occurred in 2/3 test eyes by the 48-h scoring interval and in the remaining test eye by the 72-h scoring interval. An additional ocular finding of quizartinib remaining in the eye was noted in all test animals.
Quizartinib is considered to be a mild irritant based on Kay and Calandra Evaluation Criteria
Classified as a nonirritant based on EEC Ocular Evaluation Criteria
Classified as equivocal based on FDA Evaluation Criteria due to a single animal with conjunctival redness of 2 at the 24- and 48-h scoring intervals

Dermal Irritation Toxicity
Report Title: An Acute Skin Irritation Study of AC220 by Dermal Administration in Rabbits
Study Number: 20079156 (eCTD 4.2.3.7.7)	GLP Compliance: Yes
L458: **Test system:** Rabbit/NZW; 3 males administered a single dermal dose of 0.57 g/site for 4 hours with examination at 1 hour after patch removal and 24, 14, 48, and 72 hours after patch application.
L459: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L460: - Quizartinib produced erythema at 3/3 test sites at the 1-h scoring interval. Dermal irritation resolved completely at 2/3 test sites by the 48-h scoring interval and at the remaining test site by the 72-h scoring interval.
- Quizartinib considered to be a slight skin irritant in rabbits according to the EPA-FIFRA Dermal Irritation Descriptive Classification Criteria.
- The calculated Primary Irritation Index for the test article was 0.83. According to the FDA Dermal Evaluation Criteria and the EEC Dermal Evaluation Criteria, quizartinib is considered to be a nonirritant.
