L1507: ## **8.1.5. Supportive Studies in Relapsed/Refractory AML – Study AC220-002**
L1508: Study AC220-002 was an open-label, Phase 2 study of the efficacy and safety of quizartinib monotherapy in subjects with R/R AML with and without *FLT3*-ITD activating mutations. The study was divided into 2 stages. The exploratory stage included the first 60 *FLT*-ITD (+) subjects to complete the first 28-day treatment cycle, and the confirmatory stage included all remaining subjects.
L1509: In each stage, subjects were divided into 2 cohorts (approximately 120 *FLT3*-ITD (+) and 30 *FLT3*-ITD (-) subjects each) as follows:
L1510: - Cohort 1: Subjects \ge 60 years of age who relapsed after 1 first-line chemotherapy regimen (with or without consolidation) in &lt;12 months after CR1 or who were primary refractory to first-line chemotherapy
- Cohort 2: Subjects \ge 18 years of age (including subjects \ge 60 years of age) who were relapsed or were refractory after 1 second-line (salvage) regimen or who were relapsed or were refractory after HSCT
L1511: Quizartinib was administered QD as an oral solution continuously in 28-day cycles with no breaks between cycles. Initially, the dose of quizartinib was 200 mg/day. The dose was subsequently reduced to 135 mg/day (for males) or 90 mg/day (for females), because of occurrences of QTcF prolongation with the 200 mg/day dose. Subjects received up to 3 cycles of induction with quizartinib to achieve best response, and responders continued receiving quizartinib as consolidation and maintenance therapy.
L1512: NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1513: ## **Data:**
L1514: Data from the exploratory and confirmatory stages were combined for analysis (AC220-002 CSR).
L1515: A total of 333 subjects were enrolled: 157 in Cohort 1 and 176 in Cohort 2. Subjects were predominantly white (87.4%) and almost equally distributed between males (51.1%) and females (48.9%), with a median age of 63.0 years (range: 19 to 86 years). The majority (248) of subjects were *FLT3*-ITD (+), 84 subjects were *FLT3*-ITD (-), and 1 subject had unknown or indeterminate *FLT3*-ITD status.
L1516: A total of 125 *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects (50.4%) achieved a CRc, including 63 subjects (56.3%) in Cohort 1 and 62 subjects (45.6%) in Cohort 2. In *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects, the median duration of CRc was 12.1 weeks for Cohort 1 and 10.6 weeks for Cohort 2. Twenty-eight *FLT3*-ITD (-) subjects (33.3%) achieved a CRc, including 16 subjects (36.4%) in Cohort 1 and 12 subjects (30.0%) in Cohort 2. Subjects responded early in their treatment with quizartinib, with *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects achieving a CRc in approximately 4 weeks. For *FLT3*-ITD (-) subjects, the time to CRc was nearly double for Cohort 1 compared with Cohort 2 (8.1 versus 4.1 weeks, respectively).
L1517: The ability to control disease and induce remissions enabled a substantial number of subjects to undergo allo-HSCT, particularly for the younger age group in Cohort 2. Of the 112 *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects in Cohort 1, 11 subjects had HSCT and 101 subjects had no HSCT after quizartinib. Of the 136 *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects in Cohort 2, 47 subjects were able to go on to HSCT and 89 subjects did not go onto HSCT after quizartinib.
L1518: In Study AC220-002, a threshold VAF &gt;10% was used to determine *FLT3*-ITD positivity. Since the CRc rates were similar in subjects with *FLT3*-ITD VAF ≤10% (CRc range of 36% to 58%) and those with VAF &gt;10% (CRc range of 38% to 72%), while subjects with no detectable ITD mutation had lower response rates, a lower cutoff of ≤3% was chosen as the threshold for later studies (AC220-007 and AC220-A-U302).
L1519: Median OS for *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects was approximately 25 weeks for both cohorts. For the *FLT3*-ITD (-) subjects, the median OS was 19.1 weeks in Cohort 1 and 25.1 weeks in Cohort 2. The median OS was 34.1 weeks (95% CI: 26.0, 49.0) for *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects who underwent HSCT and 18.4 weeks (95% CI: 15.0, 22.7) for subjects who did not undergo HSCT in Cohort 2.
L1520: ## 8.1.6. Supplemental Data from Other Phase 1 and Phase 2 Clinical Studies in Relapsed/Refractory AML
L1521: Study CP0001 was a Phase 1, first-in-human, open-label, 3+3 dose-escalation study of quizartinib in subjects with R/R AML or previously untreated AML who were unsuitable for induction chemotherapy because of age, comorbidities, or other factors. Quizartinib oral solution was initially administered at a starting dose of 12 mg QD for 14 days followed by a
L1522: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1523: 14-day rest period (1 cycle). Subsequently, the protocol was amended to specify a continuous dosing (CD) schedule starting at 200 mg/day for 28 days (1 cycle). Nine dose escalations (from 12 to 450 mg) were achieved by using the 14-day regimen with 3 to 8 subjects per dose level. Two CD regimens were tested with 17 subjects on 200 mg/day and 8 subjects on 300 mg/day. A total of 76 subjects received treatment in the study. Subjects were mostly white (89.5%) and male (60.5%) with a median age of 59.5 years (range: 23 years to 86 years). Seventeen subjects (22.3%) were *FLT3*-ITD (+), 37 (48.7%) were *FLT3*-ITD (-) based on a 1% ITD to WT ratio, and 22 (28.9%) had unknown or indeterminate *FLT3*-ITD status. The overall response rate in the ITT Analysis Set, *FLT3*-ITD (+), *FLT3*-ITD (-), unknown or indeterminate *FLT3*-ITD status was 30.3%, 52.9%, 13.5% and 40.9%, respectively (CP0001 CSR).
L1524: Study AC220-A-J101 was a multicenter, open-label, Phase 1 study that assessed the safety and PKs of quizartinib as multiple-dose monotherapy in Japanese subjects with R/R AML. Subjects were enrolled in the study irrespective of *FLT3* mutation status (*FLT3*-ITD [+] or *FLT3*-WT). Seventeen subjects were registered, of which 16 subjects were treated. The median age of enrolled subjects was 68 years (range: 33 to 91 years), with an almost equal proportion of males and females. Of the 17 enrolled subjects, 16 were included in the Safety Analysis Set, the Efficacy Analysis Set, and the PK/PD Analysis Sets. The subjects received a dose of 20 mg/day quizartinib for the first cohort, with doses selected from among 20, 30, and 60 mg for the second and subsequent cohorts. The efficacy evaluation showed that the overall response rate was 56.3% (9 of 16 subjects), including 5 subjects (55.6%) in the 20-mg cohort, 2 subjects (66.7%) in the 30-mg cohort, and 2 subjects (50.0%) in the 60-mg cohort. Although no CR was reported in any subject, CRc was found in 6 subjects (37.5%) in total, 2 subjects (22.2%) in the 20-mg cohort, 2 subjects (66.7%) in the 30-mg cohort, and 2 subjects (50.0%) in the 60-mg cohort. Partial remission was observed in 3 subjects (18.8%) overall, all of whom in the 20-mg cohort (3 subjects [33.3%]). CRI was observed in 5 subjects (31.3%) overall, including 2 subjects (22.2%) in the 20-mg cohort, 1 subject (33.3%) in the 30 mg cohort, and 2 subjects (50.0%) in the 60-mg cohort. CRc was reported in 5 of 7 *FLT3*-ITD (+) subjects (71.4%) overall, including 1 subject (33.3%) in the 20-mg cohort, 2 subjects (100.0%) in the 30-mg cohort, and 2 subjects (100.0%) in the 60-mg cohort (AC220-A-J101 CSR).
L1525: Study AC220-A-J201 was a multicenter, open-label, single-arm, Phase 2 clinical study to assess the efficacy and safety of quizartinib monotherapy in Japanese subjects with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML who have relapsed or were found to be refractory within 6 months after initial remission induction therapy (without consideration of whether they had received HSCT or not). The initial dose of quizartinib was 30 mg/day. For subjects receiving strong CYP3A inhibitors, the initial dose was 20 mg/day. Subsequently, whether or not to increase the initial dose of 30 to 60 mg/day or 20 to 30 mg/day was determined on Cycle 1 Day 16 and Cycle 2 Day 1 or thereafter in accordance with criteria for dose increase as per protocol. For subjects who achieved CRc after the start of treatment of quizartinib, subsequently relapsed, and met the criteria for dose increase, the dose of quizartinib was increased in the same manner.
L1526: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1527: The dose of the study drug was reduced in accordance with each of the criteria for dose reduction as per protocol. A total of 37 subjects were enrolled by the CSR data cutoff date (26 Feb 2018) and were included in the analysis sets. All 37 subjects were included in the Safety Analysis Set, and 32 subjects were included in the Efficacy Analysis Set. Subjects were mostly female (59.5%), with a median age of 65 years (range: 31 to 81 years). Overall, the CRc rate was 53.8% (14 subjects; 90% CI: 36.2, 70.8). Although no CR was reported in any subject, CRi was achieved as a best response in 13 subjects (48.1%). CRp was achieved in 1 subject (3.7%). The response rate was 77.8% (21 subjects; 95% CI: 57.7%, 91.4%) (AC220-A-J201 CSR).
L1528: Study 2689-CL-2004 was an open-label, randomized, multiple-dose, Phase 2 study in subjects with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML that have relapsed or were refractory after 1 salvage regimen or HSCT. Subjects were randomly assigned to 28-day cycles of either 30 or 60 mg/day quizartinib. Seventy-six subjects were randomized, of which 74 subjects received treatment with quizartinib. Subjects were mostly white (77.6%) males (57.9%) and had a median age of 54.5 years (range: 19 to 77 years). The majority of subjects (90.8%) were *FLT3*-ITD (+). In total, 36 of the 76 randomized subjects (47.4%) achieved a best response of CRc until end of treatment. Eighteen subjects in each dose group (47.4%) achieved CRc. Median OS was 20.9 weeks and 27.3 weeks with 1 and 5 long-term (\ge 52 weeks [ie, \ge 365 days]) survivors for the 30 and 60-mg treatment arms, respectively (2689-CL-2004 CSR).

L1542: The lack of CRs in this study is consistent with the findings in the QuANTUM-R trial, and supports the concerns raised above regarding the lack of robustness and internal consistency of the efficacy findings on QuANTUM-R. The fact that the CR rate was slightly higher (although still not significantly so) in the patients who were FLT3-ITD (-) as compared to those who were FLT3-ITD (+) raises additional questions, but these are beyond the scope of this review.
L1543: Although uninterpretable in the context of this non-randomized trial, the OS for both cohorts on Study AC220-002 were similar to the outcomes for the quizartinib arm on QuANTUM-R, such that the marginal 6.5 week OS advantage on that trial (if credible; see section 8.1.4.2 for the multiple limitations to its interpretation) appears consistent with other trials using quizartinib, and the possibility that QuANTUM-R represents an under-estimation of the OS seen with this treatment is less likely.

L1545: populations in Table 64. These results were based on response at the end of cycle 2. When analyzed at the end of study, the CR rate in the 30 mg cohort was unchanged, but there was one additional CR in the 60 mg cohort.

L1549: As mentioned above with regard to Study AC220-002, the low rate of CRs in this study is consistent with the findings in QuANTUM-R, and supports the concerns raised above regarding the lack of robustness and internal consistency of the efficacy findings on QuANTUM-R. It is clear, however, across the monotherapy studies that quizartinib does have an anti-leukemic effect on the blast cells - hence, the CRc responses. While the mostly CRi component responses, in and of themselves, do not constitute clinical benefit, they serve as evidence of quizartinib's monotherapy antileukemic activity.
L1550: For Study 2689-CL-2004, there was no active control or placebo, and cross-trial comparisons between QuANTUM-R and study 2689-CL-2004 are necessarily exploratory in nature with regard to time to event endpoints such as OS. However, as noted above for trial AC220-002, OS for both cohorts were similar to the outcomes for the quizartinib arm on QuANTUM-R, such that the marginal 6.5 week OS advantage on that trial (if credible) appears consistent with other trials using quizartinib, and the possibility that QuANTUM-R represents an under-estimation of the OS seen with this treatment is less likely.
L1551: ## 8.1.8. Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials

L1574: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1575: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1576: The primary efficacy data supporting this application are the statistically significant and clinically meaningful results from the pivotal study, AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First), which demonstrate that quizartinib added to chemotherapy improves OS of subjects with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML and supports the conclusion that quizartinib compared with placebo in combination with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy and then as continuation monotherapy for up to 36 cycles is an effective treatment in this patient population.
L1577: The results from Study AC220-A-U302 are supported by early Phase 1 studies in newly diagnosed AML, where quizartinib was added to frontline standard chemotherapy, demonstrating promising clinical activity in a limited number of subjects with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML.
L1578: Evidence of quizartinib monotherapy activity is provided by the randomized, active-controlled, pivotal Phase 3 study, AC220-007, in which a 24% reduction in the risk of death during the observation period compared to salvage chemotherapy in subjects with R/R *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML, with additional Phase 2 studies providing further supportive evidence of consistent clinical activity of quizartinib in *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML. Median OS estimates were consistent across the studies, with a median OS of approximately 27 weeks in subjects treated with quizartinib 60 mg. The results were also consistent across studies with respect to rates of CRc and CR and the duration of CRc.
L1579: Quizartinib consistently demonstrated a clinical benefit across the 10 clinical studies in AML, including 2 randomized controlled studies, both in combination with standard chemotherapy regimens and as a single agent, and support the conclusion that quizartinib provides a clinically meaningful improvement for patients with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML, a fatal disease with poor prognosis and continued high unmet medical need.
L1580: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1581: ## Methods
L1582: ## Issues with clinical development program
L1583: Prior to Study AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First), the Applicant investigated the safety and effectiveness of quizartinib in R/R AML in Study AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R). This study was discussed at ODAC due to trial conduct and safety issues. A major limitation of this trial was that it was open-label. There were notable imbalances between the arms, calling into question the validity of the OS results. Furthermore, QT prolongation was identified as a significant safety risk.
L1584: ## Issues with the pivotal (QuANTUM-First) trial design
L1585: The FDA defines maintenance therapy for AML as an extended but time-limited course of treatment given after achievement of CR with the objective of reducing the risk of relapse beyond the period of treatment (Guidance for Industry: Acute Myeloid Leukemia, 2022). Presently, there are no drugs approved as maintenance therapies for AML. While Onureg was approved for continued treatment of patients with AML who achieve CR or CRI, this is not considered a maintenance indication (Jen 2022). Levis et al (2019) reviewed post-HSCT trials of FLT3 inhibitors, noting that no trial has established the necessity of maintenance therapy in this setting. QuANTUM-First did not re-randomize patients to assess the effect of maintenance therapy. Consequently, the evidence for quizartinib maintenance therapy is limited and based purely on post-hoc, non-randomized comparisons.
L1586: ## *Patient Population and Primary Endpoints*
L1587: Two randomized, controlled trials were submitted in support of this NDA, QuANTUM-First and QuANTUM-R. QuANTUM-First was a randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind trial in newly diagnosed patients and is the only randomized trial in the population for which an indication is currently sought by the Applicant. As such, QuANTUM-First serves as an adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation into the effects of adding quizartinib to standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy followed by maintenance monotherapy. FDA analysis of the primary endpoint of OS for this study revealed a HR of 0.78 (95% CI 0.62, 0.98; p=0.0324) as discussed above. The median OS in the quizartinib arm was 31.9 months (95% CI 21.0, NE) and 15.1 months (95% CI 13.2, 26.2) in the control arm, for a 16.8-month difference in median OS between arms.
L1588: As discussed above, QuANTUM-R was randomized open label trial for R/R FLT3-ITD AML. FDA analysis of the primary endpoint of QuANTUM-R revealed an OS HR of 0.77 (95% CI 0.59, 0.99; p=0.019). The median OS in the quizartinib arm was 26.9 weeks (95% CI 23.1, 31.0) and 20.4 weeks (95% CI 17.0, 25.2) in the control arm, for a 6-week difference in median OS between the arms. The credibility of the OS results for QuANTUM-R were plagued by uncertainties that have been described above. Given these concerns and the questionable clinical utility of a 6-week extension in median OS versus salvage chemotherapy, it was not thought that QuANTUM-R represented substantial evidence of effectiveness for the treatment of patients with R/R FLT3-mutated AML.
L1589: ## *Secondary and Other Endpoints*
L1590: FDA analysis of the per protocol EFS on QuANTUM-First was not statically significant with a HR of 0.92 (95% CI 0.75, 1.11; p=0.2371). However, sensitivity analysis showed that the EFS analysis was highly dependent on the time window allowed for count recovery before considering a response as induction treatment failure. Review of CR rates between arms was remarkably
L1591: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1592: similar with CR rates of 55% on both treatment arms. However, the duration of CR was 38.6 and 12.4 months in the quizartinib versus placebo arms, respectively.
L1593: FDA's analysis of the key secondary endpoint of QuANTUM-R revealed a 2.3-week difference in median EFS between the quizartinib arm and control arm for a HR of 0.9 (95% CI 0.71, 1.16). This was not statistically significant, with a p-value of 0.1138. CR rates on QuANTUM-R were 4% on the quizartinib arm and 1% on the control arm, and CR/CRh rates were 11% on the quizartinib arm and 3% on the control arm. These CR rates are similar to those seen in the non-randomized earlier phase studies AC220-002 and 2689-CL-2004, with CR rates of 2 and 3% respectively.
L1594: ## *Subpopulations*
L1595: As discussed above, analysis of subpopulations for QuANTUM-First generally showed benefit with quizartinib treatment in most groups investigated. Only the small subgroup with favorable risk cytogenetics suggested better outcomes on the placebo arm. Post-hoc analyses of the effect of quizartinib in the maintenance phase suggest that patients who receive HSCT may not derive any further benefit from quizartinib maintenance therapy.
L1596: Any analysis of subgroups in QuANTUM-R is restricted by the limitations described for the primary analysis of the ITT population above. As described in detail, the OS results for QuANTUM-R appear to be driven primarily by the substratum of patients preselected for LDAC. There was a striking imbalance between the rates of subsequent HSCT in this particular stratum (23% in the quizartinib arm and none on the LDAC arm).
L1597: ## *Dose and Dose-Response*
L1598: Since the OS results on study QuANTUM-First and QuANTUM-R cannot be used as response assessment (CR rates were equivalent on treatment arms of QuANTUM-First and CR rates in the R/R trials were low across the development program), an assessment of dose-response is not possible at this time.
L1599: ## *Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects*
L1600: The durability of CR on QuANTUM-First is mentioned above. Since the number of patients on quizartinib arm who achieved a CR on the Study QuANTUM-R was so small (4%), an analysis of time to response and/or durability response is not informative. The same is true for CR rates across the remaining trials in the clinical development program.
L1601: ## *Other Relevant Benefits*
L1602: The applicant points to CRc, defined as CR+ CRi+ CRp as evidence of clinical benefit across multiple trials in Table 65 and Table 66 above. It is noted that CRi and CRp are not considered evidence of clinical benefit in the treatment of AML (Jen 2018). The lack of equivalency of CR as compared to CRi and CRp in the context of cytotoxic therapies was reported in a cohort of over 6000 patients with AML treated across multiple countries and centers (Walter 2010), and absent any additional data using targeted therapies specifically the equivalency of these endpoints remains unknown. However, the statistically significant OS result from QuANTUM-R, the demonstration of monotherapy activity in terms of modest CR and CRh rates across R/R AML trials (Study AC220-007, AC220-002, and 2689-CL-2004), and the demonstration of moderate CRc rates across monotherapy trials (Table 66) are sufficient to provide confirmatory evidence that quizartinib has activity in *FLT3*-ITD AML.
L1603: ## 8.1.9. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L1604: ## **Data:**
L1605: Study AC220-A-U302 provides the primary data to support efficacy claims under this NDA; see Section 8.1.2 for details. Additional studies of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy and as monotherapy in newly diagnosed or R/R AML provide additional supportive data (see Sections 8.1.4.2, 8.1.5, and 8.1.6).
L1606: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1607: Study AC220-A-U302 demonstrates that selective and potent inhibition of *FLT3*-ITD mutant signaling results in a significant improvement in survival when combined with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy, and then as continuation monotherapy for up to 36 cycles in adult *FLT3*-ITD (+) newly diagnosed AML subjects aged between 18 and 75 years old. Considering the high-risk population enrolled in Study AC220-A-U302, quizartinib is addressing a significant unmet medical need for patients with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML. This is particularly the case for older subjects (\ge 60 years), notably those who are eligible for intensive therapy, for whom current treatment options remain unsatisfactory, with a lack of randomized data to inform on appropriate treatment approaches.
L1608: Study AC220-A-U302 is the first and only global, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase 3 study that has demonstrated a survival benefit specifically in subjects with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML, a population associated with an inferior survival with chemotherapy alone. The study compared the effect of quizartinib versus placebo (administered with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy, then administered as continuation monotherapy for up to 36 cycles) on the primary endpoint of OS in adult subjects with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML. At the time of initiation of this study, chemotherapy with or without allo-HSCT was the standard for the treatment of AML; hence, placebo along with standard combination chemotherapy was used as the comparator. The study population is representative of the target population of patients with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML, and demographics and
L1609: disease characteristics of subjects in Study AC220-A-U302 were well balanced between the 2 treatment arms.
L1610: The study met its primary endpoint by demonstrating a statistical significance in OS of quizartinib compared with placebo with an HR (95% CI) of 0.776 (0.615, 0.979), corresponding to an estimated reduction in the risk of death of 22.4% in favor of the quizartinib arm. The median OS (95% CI) was 31.9 months (21.0, not estimable [NE]) in the quizartinib arm versus 15.1 months (13.2, 26.2) in the placebo arm, resulting in prolongation of median OS by 16.8 months compared with standard chemotherapy (Table 26). The survival rates at 1 year (67.4% versus 57.7%) and 2 years (54.7% versus 44.7%) also favored the quizartinib arm. The results of the preplanned supplemental and sensitivity analyses of OS were consistent with those of the primary OS analysis, showing a benefit of quizartinib, as demonstrated by consistent HRs (95% CI) of 0.774 (0.614, 0.975) for the unstratified OS analysis and 0.752 (0.562, 1.008) for the OS analysis censored at the start date of the conditioning regimen for allo-HSCT (Table 27). An improvement in OS was observed across most prespecified subgroups in favor of quizartinib. These results are considered clinically meaningful improvements, given the poor prognosis and the high unmet medical need. Furthermore, the E-R analysis indicated that the proposed dosing regimen in Study AC220-A-U302 is appropriate.
L1611: With regard to the secondary endpoint, EFS, although the primary analysis of EFS based on the definition of ITF as failure to achieve CR within 42 days of the start of the last cycle of induction chemotherapy (FDA, 2020) was not statistically significant, the sensitivity analysis based on CR in the Induction Phase using the protocol-defined 56-day window showed a clear benefit in favor of quizartinib as demonstrated by an HR (95%CI) of 0.818 (0.669, 0.999)(Table 37). Given that the protocol was designed to assess ITF up to 56 days from the start of the last induction cycle, the sensitivity analysis is consistent with the OS result and supports the overall conclusion on the efficacy of quizartinib.
L1612: Because the primary analysis for EFS was not significant, further statistical testing of other secondary endpoints was stopped. Analyses of secondary and exploratory endpoints showed that while the rates of CR were similar between treatment arms, a prespecified exploratory analysis of RFS in subjects who achieved a CR at the end of Induction Phase showed that subjects in the quizartinib arm had 3 times longer median RFS (39.3 months) than those in the placebo arm (13.6 months), resulting in a 38.7% relative risk reduction of relapse or death (Table 65). This benefit in RFS was maintained over time, and RFS rates are higher in the quizartinib arm compared with the placebo arm (73.4% versus 52.4% at 12 months and 57.8% versus 40.9% at 24 months, respectively). Furthermore, in a post hoc analysis in subjects who achieved a CR, quizartinib delayed relapse compared to placebo with lower CIR at 24 months (31.2% vs. 43.3%, respectively) and at 36 months (33.8% vs. 45.1%, respectively) from randomization (Figure 18). Additional exploratory analyses showed that the median (95% CI) duration of CR was approximately 3 times longer in subjects in the quizartinib arm (38.6 [21.9, NE] months) versus the placebo arm (12.4 [8.8, 22.7] months). The Kaplan-Meier plot of the duration of CR shows sustained separation of the curves for up to 3 years. There were no
L1613: notable differences in the demographics and baseline disease characteristics of subjects who achieved a CR between the 2 treatment arms (eCTD M2.7.3 Tables 14.1.2.1.2 and 14.1.3.1.1.3).
L1614: Taken together, these data demonstrated that quizartinib combined with chemotherapy followed by continuation monotherapy improves the OS of newly diagnosed subjects with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML. The benefit in OS is further supported by clinically meaningful improvement in RFS, the longer duration of remission, and the lower CIR in CR subjects treated with quizartinib.
L1615: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1616: We agree with the Applicant's assessment. QuANTUM-First demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvement in the primary endpoint of overall survival. This endpoint reflects both the long-term safety and efficacy of quizartinib treatment in newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD AML. Moreover, the KM plot of OS in Figure 2 above shows the treatment arms plateauing with a persistent difference, suggesting that the benefit to OS demonstrated during the Study has a continuing effect on long-term survival. While the per protocol EFS analysis was not statistically significant, sensitivity analyses conducted with an induction assessment window in line with study conduct did show benefit. Analysis of other secondary and exploratory endpoints showed a consistent effect whereby the addition of quizartinib treatment to standard therapy extended the duration of CR responses and improved RFS.
L1617: The results of FDA analyses of data from QuANTUM-First, including unadjusted and adjusted analyses of OS and RFS, suggest inconclusive evidence regarding use of quizartinib as maintenance therapy after HSCT. Note that this conclusion is based on exploratory post-hoc analyses; a more formal analysis is not possible since QuANTUM-First was inadequately designed to isolate the effect of maintenance therapy. Thus, the evidence was inadequate to support positive benefit-risk for patients post-HSCT. According to Levis et al, no *FLT3* TKI has definitively demonstrated improved efficacy for continued treatment following HSCT (Levis, 2019). Based on the lack of a demonstrated improvement in OS in patients post-HSCT, and the potential for harm (HR &gt; 1), we recommend a Limitation of Use in the prescribing information stating that quizartinib is not indicated as maintenance monotherapy following allogeneic HSCT. Although evaluations of maintenance post-consolidation chemotherapy are also limited by the exploratory post-hoc nature of the analyses, the results are compelling enough clinically (HR 0.4 for non-HSCT patients) that we recommend inclusion of maintenance monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy in the indication statement.
L1618: ## 8.2. Review of Safety

L1627: Source: eCTD 2.7.4, Table 7.1
L1628: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1629: The characterization of the safety profile of quizartinib in adult subjects with *FLT3-ITD* (+) newly diagnosed AML is primarily based on data from Study AC220-A-U302. Since this is a placebo-controlled study, the results allow for an unbiased comparison of the safety profile of quizartinib in combination with standard chemotherapy with that of chemotherapy alone. The safety database is further augmented by inclusion of an All AML Pool that provides an additional 816 subjects with AML that were treated with quizartinib, of whom 404 subjects also received quizartinib at doses in the range of 30 to 60 mg QD. Studies in healthy volunteers and subjects with hepatic impairment provide supporting information for the assessment of safety profile of quizartinib. As such, the safety database allows for a comprehensive assessment of the safety profile of quizartinib at the recommended dosing regimen and at higher quizartinib doses, including detection and characterization of common AEs, to adequately inform on appropriate toxicity management, and the evaluation of the overall benefit-risk in the proposed indication.
L1630: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1631: FDA agrees with the Applicant's approach to safety data. As stated above, the characterization of common AEs for the proposed indication is based on Study AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First). This study is a double-blind, placebo-controlled study that allows for assessment of AEs that develop with quizartinib use in the context of intensive induction and consolidation chemotherapy. The Applicant also notes that their development program and safety database include a total of 1081 patients with AML that were treated quizartinib at various dose levels,
L1632: alone and in combination with chemotherapy, to further inform the incidence of severe but less frequently seen AEs.
L1633: The efficacy evaluation for QuANTUM-First in the USPI will be presented at the time of the protocol specified final analysis (August 13, 2021); therefore, the safety analysis for purposes of the USPI will also be presented from that timepoint. The Applicant submitted an updated safety dataset using a data cutoff date of (April 13, 2022). The updated dataset did not contribute significant differences in adverse events, AEs resulting in death, dose reductions or interruptions, or dose discontinuations due to AEs.
L1634: ## 8.2.1. Safety Review Approach
L1635: ## Data:
L1636: AEs were coded using the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA), Version 24.0 and were assigned grades (severity) based on the NCI Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE) v4.03. The lower-level terms from each study's database were mapped in the integrated database to the MedDRA hierarchy. A TEAE was defined as a new AE or an AE with worsening grade occurring on or after Day 1 of study drug treatment until 30 days after the last dose of study drug. Related AEs occurring more than 30 days after the last dose of study drug were also considered TEAEs. AEs collected before the start of study drug treatment were not considered TEAEs.
L1637: Different categories of TEAE (eg, all grade and Grade 3 /4 TEAE, related TEAE, serious TEAE, TEAE associated with fatal outcome, quizartinib discontinuation and interruption) were evaluated for the pivotal study AC220-A-U302 and the All AML Pool, with focus on the 30 to 60 mg quizartinib dose group.
L1638: For the comprehensive evaluation of the safety profile of quizartinib, predefined MedDRA searches for specific categories of events of interest were conducted. The searches, defined in Table 69, were selected based on the quizartinib safety experience in previous studies, TEAEs/ treatment-emergent serious AEs (TESAEs) expected to occur with treatment with the chemotherapeutic agents used during the Induction and Consolidation Phases of Study AC220-A-U302 and certain other studies, and the reported AEs associated with treatment using other FLT3 inhibitors.
L1639: In addition to TEAEs, safety laboratory, vital signs and ECG data were evaluated. Subgroup analyses of TEAE and ECG data according to age, sex, race, ethnic origin, geographic region, duration of exposure, and concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitors and QT-prolonging medications were done, to evaluate the safety of quizartinib in specific subject subpopulations. Mild or moderate hepatic impairment and renal impairment were not associated with significant effect on quizartinib exposure (see Table 11); therefore analysis of safety data in subgroups of subjects with hepatic and renal impairment was not performed.
L1640: **Table 69 – Predefined MedDRA Search Criteria for Specific Events of Interest**
L1641: Event	Definition
QT prolongation and potentially associated events	All PTs from TdP/QT prolongation SMQ (broad) plus PTs fall, presyncope, agonal rhythm, arrhythmia, cardiac flutter, paroxysmal arrhythmia, and death
Cardiac failure	All PTs from Cardiac failure SMQ (narrow) plus PTs cardiomegaly, diastolic dysfunction, left ventricular dysfunction, cardiomyopathy, cardiotoxicity, cytotoxic cardiomyopathy, and ejection fraction decreased
IHD	All PTs from Myocardial infarction SMQ (narrow)
Other ischaemic heart disease SMQ (narrow)
Hepatotoxicity	All PTs from:
Cholestasis and jaundice of hepatic origin SMQ (narrow)
Hepatic failure, fibrosis and cirrhosis and other liver damage-related conditions SMQ (narrow)
Hepatitis, non-infectious SMQ (narrow)
Liver related investigations, signs, and symptoms SMQ (narrow)
L1642: Source: eCTD 2.7.4 Table 2.1.
L1643: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1644: The safety review approach is considered comprehensive and appropriate to identify and quantify the safety profile of quizartinib in subjects with newly diagnosed AML at the proposed dosing regimen.
L1645: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1646: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's safety review approach. We again note that TEAEs from Study AC220-A-U302 will form the basis for assessment of common AEs for the proposed indication. Study AC220-A-U302 was a randomized placebo-controlled trial and contained 87% (265/303) of all frontline AML patients from the total safety pool. The pooled analyses are used for signal seeking for other important AEs.

L1654: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1655: The size and composition of the safety database allows for a comprehensive assessment of safety in AML subjects in general and specifically in the intended population of newly diagnosed AML subjects that are *FLT3*-ITD (+). In Study AC220-A-U302 the total subject exposure was longer in the quizartinib compared to the placebo arm. In addition, it is noteworthy that a sizeable proportion of subjects in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to 60 mg dose group of the All AML Pool (30% and 17%, respectively) remained on treatment for &gt;1 year, which allowed for appropriate assessment of the long-term safety of quizartinib in this subject population.
L1656: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1657: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position that exposure in the newly diagnosed AML subjects allowed for a comprehensive assessment of safety. The Applicant notes and we concur that the median exposure was longer in the quizartinib arm of the trial. This is also reflected by a higher percentage of quizartinib patients being exposed for over 365 days and in quizartinib patients have higher mean days of treatment. This longer exposure is reflective of quizartinib patients remaining in remission longer than the placebo-treated patients and
L1658: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1659: therefore being eligible for later phases of on-study treatment. The Applicant notes that 79 subjects on the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 and an additional 36 from the All-AML pool were exposed to 30 to 60 mg quizartinib for over 365 days. This permits for an initial assessment of the long-term safety of quizartinib. However, whether this allows for complete assessment of long-term safety is a matter of analysis of the safety signals derived from these subjects. Additionally, as was noted in the clinical pharmacology section, the appropriate dose for maintenance phase of treatment was not systemically investigated.

L1675: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1676: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1677: Relevant demographic characteristics are well balanced between the quizartinib and placebo arms in Study AC220-A-U302, providing assurance with respect to the safety conclusions and the interpretation of comparisons made between treatment arms. The pivotal study and the pooled AML dataset include a substantial proportion of subjects older than 65 years, which allowed for the assessment of the safety profile of quizartinib in older age groups. The population enrolled is sufficiently diverse and representative of a broader population of adult patients with *FLT3*-ITD (+) newly diagnosed AML.
L1678: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1679: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that Study AC220-A-U302 was well balanced between the randomized arms. The population enrolled on Study AC220-A-U302 generally reflects patients with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD mutated AML that are fit for treatment with intensive chemotherapy. It is notable that the median age of subjects enrolled was 56 years for both arms and that 40% of enrolled subjects were age \ge 60 years. The racial composition of the study was 60% white, 29% Asian, 10% other (including not collected per local regulations), and 1% Black. While this racial composition is generally consistent with the geographic regions in which the trial was conducted, it is unclear what the final breakdown might have been if country specific privacy laws had allowed for the racial reporting of all enrolled subjects. It is notable that the reported enrollment of Black/African American subjects was (1%) on study AC220-A-U302, (3%) in the total AML quizartinib safety population, and (31%) of subjects enrolled to the healthy volunteer study. The total AML quizartinib safety population demographics are similar to those of Study AC220-A-U302. Some notable features include slightly older population with median age of 58 years old and 47% of patients age \ge 60 years, higher percentage of white subjects (72%) and a lower percentage of Asian subjects (16.3%), and wider distribution of *FLT3*-ITD VAF levels (based on inclusion of patients with *FLT3*-WT).
L1680: ## **Adequacy of the safety database:**
L1681: ## **Data:**
L1682: See preceding sections.
L1683: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1684: The size and composition of the safety database allows for a comprehensive assessment of the safety of quizartinib in the intended population of newly diagnosed AML patients that are *FLT3*-ITD (+) and in specific patient subgroups.
L1685: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1686: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position. The number of patients and the duration of follow up included in the safety database is adequate to provide a reasonable estimate of
L1687: adverse reactions over time for the indicated population.
L1688: ## 8.2.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L1689: ## Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L1690: ## **Data:**
L1691: The Applicant's quality control and quality assurance activities, data management, and statistical analysis of the study data were implemented per the Applicant's applicable GCP standard operating procedures. Monitors designated by the Applicant to ensure compliance of the investigators and other site staff members involved in this study were granted direct access to the source documents maintained at individual study sites and verified that CRF data were accurately reported and consistent with the source data. As applicable, CROs and central laboratories implemented data quality control activities based on their relevant in house standard operating procedures.
L1692: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1693: No material concerns were identified with respect to the quality and integrity of the submitted datasets or individual case narratives. Data sets and case narratives were sufficiently complete to allow for a thorough review of safety. Furthermore, no data integrity concerns were reported in the course of site monitoring by the Applicant or its designees and data captured in CRF and AE databases were consistent.
L1694: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1695: The quality of the safety data submitted was adequate to allow substantial primary review.
L1696: ## Categorization of Adverse Events
L1697: ## **Data:**
L1698: The safety profile observed with quizartinib was evaluated on the basis of the following:
L1699: - Frequency, type, severity, and causal relationship of TEAEs to study treatment
- Frequency of deaths, treatment-emergent SAEs, and other clinically significant TEAEs (including AEs associated with study drug discontinuation, dose reduction, and/or study drug interruption, and AESIs and other select AEs)
- AESI for the study includingQTcF prolongation Grade \ge 3, either serious or non-serious and whether or not causally related
- Combined elevations of aminotransferases (AT) and bilirubin
L1700: - TEAEs identified based on predefined MedDRA searches for specific events of interest (Table 69)
- Frequency and type of TEAEs in subgroups according to key intrinsic and extrinsic factors (eg, age, sex, race, ethnic origin, duration of exposure, geographic region, and concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitors)
- Changes in laboratory variables, vital signs and ECGs, with particular attention to abnormalities of CTCAE Grade 3 or 4.
L1701: See also Section 8.2.1.
L1702: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1703: The categorization of AEs was considered appropriate for evaluating the safety data in patients with AML in general and specifically in the intended population with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML.
L1704: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1705: FDA agreed with mapping of reported terms to preferred terms per MedDRA 24.0. Terms that referred directly to relapse, persistence of disease, or progression of AML were excluded from FDA's analyses. TEAEs were assessed by frequency (event per patient) and maximum grade reported.
L1706: FDA reviewed the Applicant's grouped terms as above but also developed a list of group terms based on PTs that were reported in the AML ISS pool (see Appendix 19.5 for a table of FDA grouped terms used throughout FDA safety analyses unless otherwise noted). FDA assessed laboratory abnormalities as both reported AEs and by shifts from baseline labs. Since all patients in Study AC220-A-U302 presented with acute leukemia, most patients had some level of abnormal baseline labs induced by the disease process. Therefore, analysis anchored from this abnormal baseline would not identify labs that normalized with control of the acute leukemia but later became abnormal again. Therefore, analysis of both lab shifts and lab abnormalities reported as TEAEs were evaluated to identify abnormal signals.
L1707: ## Routine Clinical Tests
L1708: ## Data:
L1709: Clinical tests for safety assessment included clinical laboratory evaluations (hematology, biochemistry, coagulation, and urinalysis), vital signs and ECGs. The schedule of assessments in the protocol specified the time points at which laboratory evaluations were conducted. Per protocol, abnormal laboratory, vital signs, or ECG values considered clinically significant by the investigator were to be reported as TEAEs.
L1710: ECG in the study were obtained in triplicate and average of triplicate readings were used for the central ECG analysis. The investigator could use ECG obtained from the study-supplied ECG machine to guide initial decisions regarding dose reduction, interruption, or escalation until the average analysis from the central ECG laboratory became available. Once the analysis from the central ECG laboratory had been received, it should be used to guide dosing decisions.
L1711: Monitoring for hypokalemia at least 3 times weekly was performed during the Induction and Consolidation Phases of the study.
L1712: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1713: The clinical monitoring of subject safety (methods and time points) described in the protocol is considered to be reasonable and adequate for the population, disease, and targeted indication being investigated.
L1714: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1715: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's position. See the discussion concerning laboratory AEs versus laboratory value shift analysis in the FDA comment above.

L1767: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1768: AML disease progression was the most common cause of death in the quizartinib studies. Overall, 9.8% and 21.8% of subjects, respectively, died due to disease progression within 30 days and more than 30 days of last dose of quizartinib. AEs were the second most common cause of death within 30 days after last dose of quizartinib, and infections and infestations were the most frequent category of fatal AEs.
L1769: In the pivotal study, AC220-A-U302, on-treatment deaths were all deaths that occurred within 30 days of the last dose of study medication. In accordance with reporting requirements for deaths in the study, the primary cause of on-treatment death was recorded as an AE. In Study AC220-A-U302, AML disease progression was not required to be reported as an AE per protocol; therefore, the incidence of TEAE of AML progression and/or relapse is much lower than in the earlier studies in the development program. In the AC220-A-U302 study, the most frequently reported types of TEAEs with an outcome of death were infections (septic shock and sepsis). These fatal infections predominantly occurred during the Induction and Consolidation Phases when subjects were also receiving intensive chemotherapy, with low incidence of fatal infections in the Continuation Phase.
L1770: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1771: In Study AC220-A-U302, a numerically higher incidence of deaths within 30 days of the start of study drug was observed in the quizartinib arm compared with the placebo arm. The majority of these early deaths in the quizartinib arm were also due to infections (sepsis and septic shock).
L1772: The incidence of cardiac events, including cardiac arrhythmias, associated with fatal outcome was low. Two subjects from the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 experienced cardiac arrest that were associated with recorded ventricular fibrillation on ECG (reported as a separate TEAE for one of the subjects) and occurred in the context of Grade 3 or 4 hypokalemia. One of the subjects was successfully resuscitated by cardioversion, however the second subject died as a result of the event. An additional subject in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 died in his sleep 10 days after the last dose of quizartinib with no cause of death identified and no significant QTcF prolongation during the study.
L1773: In addition to the cases from Study AC220-A-U302, a comprehensive review of all potential cardiac arrhythmia events from the entire quizartinib development program revealed 1 additional case of cardiac arrest, which occurred at higher quizartinib doses in subjects with some evidence of QTcF prolongation prior to the event. The event of fatal cardiac arrest occurred in a subject in the setting of sepsis 4 days after the starting quizartinib dose of 90 mg was increased to 135 mg and the subject was on a concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor. The subject's QTcF interval had increased from baseline of 408 ms to a maximum of 496 ms 4 days before the cardiac arrest but was reportedly 471 ms the day before death.
L1774: Other TEAE with fatal outcome were infrequent at 30 to 60 mg dose of quizartinib, with very low incidence of major hemorrhagic events, including intracranial hemorrhage.
L1775: Early mortality is a known complication in newly diagnosed AML subjects and can be attributed to several confounding factors, such as age, frailty, organ dysfunction, underlying disease (cytogenetic abnormality), comorbidities, and complications from chemotherapy. Bleeding and infections are the most common complications of standard chemotherapy and can result in increased risk of treatment-related early mortality during or after induction therapy. In Study AC220-A-U302, the OS Kaplan-Meier (KM) curves show an early crossing attributed to early deaths. The observed incidence rate of early deaths in the study is small and in line with reported early mortality rates in patients with newly diagnosed AML receiving standard chemotherapy (Sasaki, 2022). OS captures all-cause mortality during the study and the imbalance in deaths seen early in the study does not persist over the course of quizartinib treatment.
L1776: The OS KM curves show a clear and sustained separation in favor of the quizartinib arm, with an early crossing attributed to early deaths. Early mortality is a known complication in newly diagnosed AML subjects and can be attributed to several confounding factors, such as age, frailty, organ dysfunction, underlying disease (cytogenetic abnormality), comorbidities, and complications from chemotherapy. Bleeding and infections are the most common complications of standard chemotherapy and can result in increased risk of treatment-related
L1777: early mortality during or after induction therapy. Overall survival captures all-cause mortality during the study and the quizartinib arm has demonstrated a substantial improvement in OS over the placebo arm. The early imbalance in deaths does not persist over the course of quizartinib treatment and the overall conclusion on efficacy is not impacted.
L1778: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1779: FDA performed an assessment of deaths, based on review of individual patient narratives, in patients enrolled on Study AC220-A-U302 that occurred within 30 days of the last dose of study drug, either placebo or quizartinib. In comparison to the Applicant, the FDA used simplified categories for root cause of death including TEAE treatment related, AML progressive disease, and TEAE related to transplant. We would have also considered intercurrent illness/event as a root cause of death. However, we did not identify any deaths on Study AC220-A-U302 that we would have categorized as such. Table 77 below shows the summary of FDA adjudicated deaths between arms.
L1780: **Table 77: FDA Root Cause of Death**
L1781: FDA Root Cause of death	Quizartinib
(n=265)	Placebo
(n=268)
TEAE treatment related	26 (10%)	14 (5%)
AML progressive
disease/Relapse	2 (0.8%)	10 (3.7%)
TEAE related to transplant	4 (1.5%)	1 (0.4%)
L1782: Source: FDA Analysis
L1783: In making root cause determination, FDA did not assign the AE to either the chemotherapy, if recently given, or study drug (placebo or quizartinib). Instead, the determination was made that the AE was related to recent protocol therapy (not to progressive/ relapsed disease, transplant related TEAE, or intercurrent illness) and that comparison between treatment arms would be used to assess for potential contribution from quizartinib treatment. This is in contrast to the Applicant's approach to adjudication where their physician reviewers attempted to assign the AE to either study drug and/or chemotherapy. When comparing root cause assignments, FDA considered the Applicant's root cause in agreement with FDA's use of TEAE treatment-related if they attributed the AE to either chemotherapy or study drug. FDA did not agree with the Applicants root cause of death in the 27 cases listed in Table 78 below. We note that in 25 of these cases, the Applicant attributed the root cause to an AE secondary to AML. For these cases, FDA considered these events to be related to the treatment, since most were complications from cytopenia such as infections and bleeding events and these cytopenias were greatly exacerbated by recent treatments with chemotherapy and study drugs without mention of refractory or relapsed disease. We would also note that the Applicant was inconsistent with the approach to such cases, with cases of sepsis-related deaths occurring during induction alternatively being attributed to an adverse event from chemotherapy or as
L1784: disease-related AE without clear reason for the different attribution.

L1791: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
L1792: {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1793: Quizartinib	122	Consolidation	AE treatment related	Adverse Event: Related/secondary to AML Disease	Pulmonary sepsis
L1794: Source: FDA Analysis
L1795: *Note that Study Day is measured from the first day of quizartinib, not from the first day of chemotherapy
L1796: There were also two cases that the Applicant was unable to judge the cause of death.
L1797: Subject (b)(6), was a 51-year-old male assigned to the quizartinib treatment. He presented back to hospital at the end of consolidation cycle 1 with chest pain, SOB, and cyanosis that was diagnosed as a PE and treated as such. However, the Applicant's physician reviewers noted that a definitive diagnosis of PE was not made, and the subject died several days later after their status worsened. Due to the proximity to study treatment, FDA considered this death treatment related. Although, FDA agrees that the proximal cause of death was not clear from narrative, PE could have been consistent with the described presentation.
L1798: Subject (b)(6), was a 69-year-old male assigned to the quizartinib treatment. The subject died in his sleep at end of consolidation cycle 1, 10 days after last dose of quizartinib and 24 days after last dose of HiDAC. This subject had not had issues with QTc prolongation while on study. Due to the proximity to chemotherapy and quizartinib treatment, FDA considered this treatment related.
L1799: FDA noticed an unexpected signal in the early deaths on the quizartinib arm while reviewing patient narratives. We noted that there was an overrepresentation of Russian sites in the early induction deaths in general and on the quizartinib arm in particular. While the overall induction on-treatment death rate was 6.1% for all patients on both arms of Study AC220-A-U302, Russian sites had an on-treatment induction death rate of 32% (6/19) and randomized induction death rate of 37% (7/19; one patient died after randomization but prior to receiving treatment with quizartinib). Most of these deaths were on the quizartinib arm (5/6 and 6/7, respectively). When FDA reviewed the death narratives, 5/6 induction on-treatment deaths described rapidly progressive septic shock unresponsive to initial treatment. We also noted that the choice of empiric antibiotics did not reflect the standard of care generally employed in the treatment of neutropenic patients. It is unclear if a more traditional choice of antibiotics might have changed the outcomes for these cases. The remaining case was hemorrhagic stroke that occurred in a patient after a second cycle of induction chemotherapy.

L1819: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1820: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1821: In Study AC220-A-U302, more subjects in the quizartinib arm experienced TESAEs, compared to the placebo arm (143 subjects [54.0%] and 123 subjects [45.9%], respectively). Quizartinib is associated with development of myelosuppression in treated subjects, which is a risk factor for the development of infections. In the AC220-A-U302 study, infections (pneumonia, septic shock, and sepsis) and blood disorders (febrile neutropenia) were the most commonly reported TESAE in the study.
L1822: Overall, the incidence of serious infections and cytopenias was generally well balanced between the 2 treatment arms, consistent with the use of induction and consolidation chemotherapy in the study. The majority of these events occurred predominantly in the Induction and Consolidation Phases of the study; however serious cytopenias and infections (pneumonia) also occurred in the Continuation Phase, reflecting the known myelosuppressive effect of quizartinib. Serious infections are a known risk for subjects with AML receiving intensive cytarabine and anthracycline chemotherapy (Baluch, 2019; Afessa, 2006).
L1823: Generally, the types of TESAEs reported in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool were consistent with those reported in Study AC220-A-U302.
L1824: SAEs reported from the pivotal study are consistent with those observed in the quizartinib AML development program, which included subjects with R/R disease, receiving quizartinib monotherapy.
L1825: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1826: FDA performed an analysis of all-grade treatment-emergent SAEs utilizing FDA grouped terms occurring in \ge 2\% of subjects on Study AC220-A-U302, which is presented in Table 81 below. The results agreed with the Applicant's analysis overall but differed in the rates of grouped events. It is notable that despite higher rate of overall TESAEs in the quizartinib arm (54% quizartinib versus 46% placebo) that there was not a strong signal in any one TESAE to account for this difference. FDA utilized criteria of occurrence in at least 5% of subjects and an event rate 2% greater on the quizartinib arm to determine TESAEs that were likely increased by quizartinib exposure beyond the high background rate of intensive chemotherapy treatment. With these criteria, only the TESAE of Febrile neutropenia was more common in quizartinib arm (11% quizartinib versus 8% placebo) than the placebo arm and included in labeling. FDA considered a lower event threshold rate of at least 2% of quizartinib-treated subjects and an event rate 2% greater on the quizartinib arm to determine TESAEs but even these criteria would not have yielded additional quizartinib associated TESAEs.

L1850: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1851: In Study AC220-A-U302 overall, infections (septic shock, pneumonia and sepsis) and cytopenias (thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and febrile neutropenia) were the most frequent events associated with study drug discontinuation. The percentage of subjects with TEAEs associated with study drug discontinuation was higher with quizartinib than with placebo; this difference was observed in all phases with the highest incidence and difference
L1852: between quizartinib and placebo seen in the Continuation Phase. The higher incidence of TEAE associated with discontinuation in the Continuation Phase is likely due to the longer duration of this phase, with accrual of events. Infections associated with quizartinib discontinuation tended to occur in the early phases of the study, whereas discontinuation due to TEAEs of cytopenias was more frequent in the Continuation Phase. In the Continuation Phase, cytopenias (thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and anemia) and gastrointestinal disorders (nausea, diarrhea, and vomiting) were the most frequent events associated with study drug discontinuation and were reported by subjects only in the quizartinib arm (AC220-A-U302 CSR Table 14.3.2.2.1).
L1853: Management of QT interval prolongation with quizartinib includes quizartinib dose modification and interruption. However, only 2 subjects (0.8%) in the quizartinib arm discontinued due to TEAEs of ECG QT prolonged.
L1854: The incidence and types of TEAEs associated with discontinuation in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool were consistent with those reported in Study AC220-A-U302.
L1855: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1856: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of study drug discontinuation events. FDA assessed TEAEs leading to study drug discontinuation in Study AC220-A-U304 with FDA group terms as shown in Table 85 below. Our analysis agreed with the Applicant's in that sepsis was the most common TEAE causing treatment discontinuation on Study AC220-A-U302. Sepsis and pneumonia were the most common TEAEs in the ISS AML Quizartinib pool causing study drug discontinuation. Per the tabulations below, sepsis as a cause of discontinuation was included in quizartinib labeling.

L1867: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1868: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1869: In Study AC220-A-U302, dose interruption and dose reduction due to TEAEs occurred more frequently in quizartinib-treated subjects. Overall, infections (pneumonia, septic shock, and sepsis) and cytopenias (neutropenia, neutrophil count decreased, thrombocytopenia, and platelet count decreased) were the most frequent events associated with study drug dose reduction and dose interruption. Infections associated with quizartinib dose reduction and interruption tended to occur in the early phases of the study, whereas discontinuation, dose reduction, or dose interruption due to TEAEs of cytopenias was more frequent in the Continuation Phase.
L1870: Management of QT interval prolongation with quizartinib includes quizartinib dose modification and interruption. Therefore, TEAEs of ECG QT interval prolonged associated with quizartinib interruption or dose reduction were reported commonly in the study (7 subjects [2.6%] and 10 subjects [3.8%], respectively).
L1871: The incidence and types of TEAEs associated with dose interruption in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool were consistent with those reported in Study AC220-A-U302. Integration of data for the All AML Pool was not performed for dose reduction, as these data were not captured consistently across all clinical studies included in the All AML Pool.
L1872: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1873: FDA conducted an analysis of TEAEs leading to dose interruptions using FDA group terms from Study AC220-A-U302 as shown in Table 87 below. This shows the results for TEAEs with a frequency of 2% or greater. Per FDA group terms, the most frequent TEAE leading to dose interruption was neutropenia in 11% of quizartinib treated patients. For labeling, we assess quizartinib related interruptions of clinical significance as those occurring in ≥2% of subjects and ≥2% greater on the quizartinib arm than the placebo. Of those listed in the table only neutropenia, myelosuppression, and thrombocytopenia were included in labeling.

L1888: ## **Significant Adverse Events**
L1889: ## Data:
L1890: The proportions of subjects with Grade 3/4 events were similar between the treatment arms. The most frequently reported Grade 3/4 TEAEs in the quizartinib arm included cytopenias (febrile neutropenia, neutropenia, neutrophil count decreased, and thrombocytopenia), hypokalemia, and infections (pneumonia), of which the events of neutropenia and neutrophil count decreased occurred at a \ge 5 percentage points higher incidence in the quizartinib arm than in the placebo arm (eCTD 2.7.4 Table 2.1.2.3).
L1891: Generally, the pattern and incidence of Grade 3/4 TEAEs in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool were consistent with those in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302. Blood cytopenias were the most frequently reported Grade 3/4 events in the All AML Pool, with no observed dose response trend. Infections (pneumonia and sepsis) were the second most frequently reported types of severe TEAE; no dose-dependent trend was noted for these TEAEs (eCTD 2.7.4 Table 2.1.2.3).
L1892: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1893: Blood cytopenias (neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, and anemia) and infections were the most common severe (Grade 3/4) TEAEs. Severe cytopenias are expected to occur in subjects receiving induction and consolidation chemotherapy, and management of these is part of routine clinical care for subjects with newly diagnosed AML. Overall, the majority of TEAEs of blood cytopenias were managed with study drug interruptions or dose reductions and few subjects discontinued quizartinib due to these events. AEs reported are described in other sections of this document.
L1894: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1895: FDA agrees with the Applicants assessment that blood cytopenias and infections were the most common severe TEAEs. FDA conducted analysis of grade 1-4 and grade 3-4 TEAEs with FDA group terms in Table 94 below. However, we note that although severe cytopenias are expected from intensive chemotherapy, the rates of cytopenias reported as severe TEAEs were higher on the quizartinib arm of this blinded study. This indicates that quizartinib worsened cytopenias beyond those induced by intensive chemotherapy alone.

L1930: TEAEs identified as adverse drug reactions (ADRs) for the Continuation Phase of Study AC220-A-U302 are described below.
L1931: The most common adverse reactions in the quizartinib arm during continuation monotherapy were neutrophil count decreased (88%), platelet count decreased (72%), hemoglobin decreased (55%), ALT increased (49%), upper respiratory tract infections (28%), nausea (23%) and diarrhea (21%). No fatal adverse reactions occurred during continuation. The most common Grade 3 or 4 adverse reactions reported in subjects with quizartinib were neutrophil count decreased (72%), platelet count decreased (24%), and hemoglobin decreased (18%).
L1932: Serious adverse reactions in the quizartinib arm during Continuation monotherapy were herpes infections (3.4%), neutropenia (2.6%), upper respiratory tract infections (1.7%), and vomiting (1.7%).
L1933: The most common adverse reactions associated with dose interruption of quizartinib during Continuation monotherapy were neutropenia (24%), thrombocytopenia (10%), and ECG QT prolonged (4.3%). The most common adverse reactions associated with dose reduction of quizartinib were neutropenia (21%), thrombocytopenia (10%), and ECG QT prolonged (5%). The most common adverse reactions associated with permanent discontinuation of quizartinib during Continuation monotherapy were thrombocytopenia (2.6%), nausea, neutropenia and decreased appetite (1.7% each).

L1953: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1954: The most common ADRs were ALT increased, cytopenias, and gastrointestinal disorders. Adverse reactions with a fatal outcome were fungal infections and cardiac arrest. Serious adverse reactions in \ge 2\% of subjects were neutropenia, fungal infections and herpes infections. Severe (Grade 3/ 4) ADRs were cytopenias (neutrophil count decreased, platelet count decreased, and hemoglobin decreased). Overall, ADRs for quizartinib were manageable by dose modification and routine clinical practice.
L1955: The profile of quizartinib ADR for the proposed indications are consistent with the overall safety profile of quizartinib determined in the development program for R/R AML.
L1956: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1957: FDA tabulations of TEAEs agreed with the Applicant's in terms of rate of any TEAE, grade 3 or 4 TEAEs, grade 5 TEAEs, and TEAEs leading to either dose discontinuations, dose reductions, or dose interruptions. Table 94 shows FDA tabulation of the most common grade 1-4 and grade 3-4 TEAEs that occurred in at least 10% of subjects on Study AC220-A-U302.

L1967: GT: group term, see Appendix 19.5
L1968: *indicates ≥2% greater on quizartinib arm
L1969: Due to the high rate of TEAEs induced by the intensive chemotherapy backbone, FDA identified those TEAEs that occurred in at least 10% of subjects and ≥2% of patients on the quizartinib arm than placebo as quizartinib-related adverse events. These are marked in Table 94 above with (*) following the preferred term and were included in labeling. As the Applicant's analysis similarly showed, the most common TEAEs were GI toxicity, infectious complications, and cytopenias. It is notable that although quizartinib increased the occurrence rate of some of the above TEAEs, many of the TEAEs either occurred at the same as or more frequently on the placebo arm. Moreover, other than QTc prolonged and neutropenia, quizartinib did not induce a significant shift to higher toxicity (higher rates of grade 3 or 4) for the common TEAEs listed above.
L1970: FDA looked at the rate of TEAEs that occurred in the monotherapy maintenance phase of protocol treatment to understand both the potential risk of treatment during this longest phase of treatment and the toxicity incurred from quizartinib in patients without the high background rate of AEs caused by active disease or intensive chemotherapy. Table 95 shows that the most common TEAE in quizartinib-treated patients during continuation therapy was neutropenia (48% grade 1-4 and 43% with grade 3-4). Other common TEAEs that were at least 2% more common with quizartinib during this phase included GI toxicity (diarrhea, nausea, abdominal pain, vomiting, decreased appetite), thrombocytopenia, upper respiratory infections, herpesvirus infections (shingles and HSV), mucositis, hypertransaminasemia, QTc prolonged, musculoskeletal pain, cough, and headache. It is interesting to note that despite the high rate of neutropenia, subjects were not at high risk of neutropenic fever or sepsis. Instead, there was an increase in milder infections such as reactivation of latent herpes viruses and a higher rate of reported upper respiratory infections.
L1971: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L1972: During the maintenance phase, analysis of TEAE by history of transplant showed that rate of individual TEAEs in quizartinib arm were an average of 8% higher if the subject had received a transplant compared to those that did not. For example, the rate of neutropenia during the maintenance phase was 51% for quizartinib-treated patients that underwent transplant but only 43% for those that had not been transplanted (Table 96). All of the listed TEAEs followed this trend except for QT prolongation, whose TEAE rate was not increased by history of transplant (10% quizartinib-HSCT vs 13% quizartinib-nonHSCT). The majority of reported TEAEs during this phase occurred during the first year of maintenance treatment.

L1999: The hematology parameters assessed were platelets, hemoglobin, WBCs, neutrophils, and lymphocytes. The blood chemistry parameters assessed were sodium, magnesium, potassium, calcium, serum creatinine, ALT, AST, total bilirubin (TBL), and ALP. Hepatic function abnormalities are presented in Table 107 and the rest of the laboratory data are summarized below.
L2000: In Study AC220-A-U302, as expected in a population of subjects with AML, many hematology parameter values were outside the normal range at baseline and during the study. For most subjects, Grade 3 or 4 values were observed for neutrophils, WBC, hemoglobin, platelets, and lymphocytes with treatment.
L2001: Approximately 15% of subjects in each treatment arm had severe hypokalemia during the study (shifts from normal potassium levels or Grade 1 hypokalemia at baseline to Grade 3 or 4 hypokalemia).
L2002: Shifts in renal function parameters (glomerular filtration rate [GFR] and creatinine clearance) were observed infrequently among subjects in both treatment arms. Approximately 4% of subjects in both treatment arms had shifts to GFR &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m2, and 2% of subjects had shifts to creatinine clearance of &lt;30 mL/min.
L2003: Similar decreases in hematology parameters, non-hepatic chemistry evaluations, and renal laboratory parameters to those observed in Study AC220-A-U302 were observed in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool.
L2004: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2005: Quizartinib has a myelosuppressive effect and shifts in hematological parameters (anemia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, lymphopenia) were observed in the pivotal study.
L2006: No clinically meaningful changes over time were found in clinical chemistry parameters in either treatment arm. Overall, shifts in these chemistry parameters were observed infrequently after treatment. Although hypokalemia is a risk factor for QT prolongation and associated arrhythmia events with quizartinib, no difference in severe hypokalemia (Grade 3 /4) was observed with quizartinib treatment relative to placebo treatment in Study AC220-A-U302
L2007: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2008: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of laboratory shifts. FDA analysis was generally in agreement with the Applicant's results. However, it is important to note that the unique situation of acute leukemia and induction of remission means that most patients present with baseline abnormalities in many labs and then develop further worsening with induction chemotherapy. High baseline labs obscure on-treatment worsening that might occur after resolution with remission attainment and hides prolonged abnormalities. Moreover, induction/consolidation chemotherapy essentially make all patients severely cytopenic. Therefore, FDA used a combination of laboratory shift analysis and laboratory values reported as TEAEs to capture laboratory adverse events induced by treatment. To ascertain which labs were worsening with quizartinib treatment above the background of intensive chemotherapy, we again used a threshold of \ge 2\% worse on the quizartinib arm compared to placebo as indicated with (*) in Table 100 below. One notable lab that did show a marked difference with quizartinib treatment was elevations of creatine kinase (25% with quizartinib versus 7% with placebo). It is interesting that creatinine kinase showed this elevation with quizartinib treatment while musculoskeletal pain was less common on the quizartinib arm overall (27% vs 32%) and only slightly more common on the quizartinib arm during maintenance (20% quizartinib versus 18% placebo). While analysis of delayed count recovery reflected some of the myelosuppression induced by quizartinib treatment, perhaps the best assessment of this effect is shown in Table 95 reflecting use of continuous quizartinib treatment given to patients in remission.

L2031: ## Electrocardiograms
L2032: ## Data:
L2033: Quizartinib prolongs QT interval in a dose-dependent and exposure-dependent manner. Regular ECG monitoring was required per study protocol in the AC220-A-U302 study. The schedule of assessments in the protocol specified the time points at which laboratory evaluations were conducted.
L2034: ECG data were obtained in triplicate and were analyzed in a central laboratory, with the central ECG reading utilized for the safety analyses. ECG matched with quizartinib PK measurements were used for the concentration-QTc E-R analysis.
L2035: More frequent assessments were performed during the Induction and Consolidation Phases of the study, with the frequency of testing reduced in the Continuation Phase. More frequent ECG recordings were required during quizartinib initiation and dose escalation.
L2036: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2037: The results of the ECG data analysis are discussed under the respective topic of QT prolongation and associated clinical events in Section 8.2.5.1.
L2038: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2039: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment. QT prolongation is further discussed below.

L2081: Source: ISS Table 7.1.1
L2082: A concentration-QTcF (C-QTcF) E-R analysis was also conducted to assess the subject characteristics that may have impacted QTc prolongation.
L2083: In Study AC220-A-U302, a direct-response maximum effect (Emax) model best described the C-QTcF relationship between quizartinib concentrations and QTcF. The median model-predicted \DeltaQTcF at Cmax,ss in subjects with newly diagnosed AML in Study AC220-A-U302 during the Continuation Phase at 30 mg and 60 mg, assuming no dose interruption or discontinuation, were 18.4 ms (90% CI: 16.3, 20.5) and 24.1 ms (90% CI: 21.4, 26.6), respectively.
L2084: The covariates that were tested in the C-QTcF model included hypokalemia, serum calcium and magnesium concentrations, age, body weight, sex, race, and use of QT-prolonging drugs, beta-blocker drugs, and anthracycline. Age and hypokalemia had an effect on the baseline QTcF. The covariate analysis on the drug effect model identified age, concurrent daunorubicin use, hypokalemia, and calcium levels as statistically significant covariates on Emax. However, interindividual variability on Emax was increased after including the covariate effects, and these effects were not seen in the graphical analysis. In addition, age, daunorubicin use, and hypokalemia effects were estimated with high uncertainty with RSE &gt;34%. As a result, these covariate effects were not retained in the model. The remaining covariates had no effect on the baseline QTcF.
L2085: Concomitant administration of QT-prolonging medications had no impact on the observed QTcF increases associated with quizartinib concentrations. More than 80% of subjects in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 used at least one concomitant medication with a 'known' risk of QT prolongation according to the Arizona Center for Education and Research on Therapeutics (AZCERT) classification, which overlapped for at least 1 day with quizartinib administration. Most common drugs in this category were antifungals (fluconazole), antiemetics (ondansetron) and antibiotics (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin; Table 106).
