L2089: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2090: Quizartinib is associated with QTc prolongation in a dose-dependent manner. QTc prolongation may increase the risk of ventricular arrhythmias or TdP. While the incidence of TEAEs of ECG QT prolonged in the study was higher in the quizartinib arm than in the placebo arm, the majority of events were asymptomatic and Grade 1 or 2, and the incidence of Grade 3 QTcF prolongation was low, demonstrating that QTcF prolongation with quizartinib is manageable with dose modification or interruption and correction of additional risk factors (eg, monitoring for and correction of electrolyte abnormalities, quizartinib dose modification for QTcF prolongation, and for concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor use). Ventricular arrhythmia events were infrequent in the study: 2 subjects (0.8%) in the quizartinib arm experienced cardiac arrest with recorded ventricular fibrillation (both during the Induction Phase), 1 with fatal outcome, in the presence of severe hypokalemia, and 1 subject died in his sleep with no cause of death identified and who had no significant QTc prolongation on ECG while on study drug. In the overall AML development program, the incidence of ventricular arrhythmia events was low at the recommended quizartinib doses.
L2091: QT prolongation is a notable risk to be mitigated. Quizartinib is a CYP3A substrate, and concomitant administration of strong CYP3A inhibitors increases quizartinib exposure. Therefore, quizartinib dose should be reduced when used concomitantly with strong CYP3A inhibitors and the study protocol allowed for the use of concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitors with appropriate quizartinib dose reduction. In addition, concomitant administration of QT-prolonging medications, which were allowed in the study as warranted in the care of AML subjects, may potentially lead to increased incidence of QT prolongation with quizartinib. However, in Study AC220-A-U302 a large proportion of subjects received QT-prolonging medications concomitantly, the QTcF analysis while receiving QT-prolonging medications was not notably different from when these same subjects were not on concomitant QT-prolonging medications.
L2092: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2093: FDA notes that preclinical characterization of quizartinib and its major metabolite AC886 suggested that it could affect the cardiac action potential. Specifically, patch clamp experiments showed that quizartinib and AC886 inhibited hERG current (I_{Kr}) and I_{Ks} currents. These experiments showed greater partial inhibition of I_{Ks} with 67% or 27% inhibition at 3 \muM for Quizartinib or AC886, respectively. This is compared to only 16% or 12% for I_{Kr} inhibition at 3 \muM of Quizartinib or AC886, respectively. To place this in perspective, the Cmax concentration in people administered 60 mg quizartinib is approximately 1 \muM for quizartinib and 0.3 \muM for the AC886 metabolite. Therefore, although inhibition of both rectifying currents was observed, it is thought that the predominant effect on cardiac repolarization would be due to the greater inhibition of the I_{Ks} current.
L2094: Most drugs that prolong the QTc interval inhibit hERG channel and the I_{Kr} current. Therefore, clinical experience to date in terms of QTc monitoring, level of risk relative to QTc interval prolongation, and the combined effect of the polypharmacy of QTc prolonging medications on the QTc interval are based on inhibition of I_{Kr}. It remains unclear how much of that experience can be extrapolated to I_{Ks} inhibitors. At least two other approved drugs have shown inhibition of the I_{Ks} current, arsenic trioxide and indapamide. Arsenic trioxide blocks both the I_{Kr} and the I_{Ks} currents and is well-known for its cardiac side effects including QT prolongation, Torsade de Pointes, and sudden death. Given the dual blockade noted for arsenic trioxide, it is hard to extrapolate the contribution toward the overall risk from I_{Ks} inhibition. Indapamide is a blood pressure medication first approved in 1983 with dual thiazide type diuretic effects and direct with vasodilatory effects. While preclinical studies have shown the I_{Ks} current inhibition and case reports have described episodes of Torsade de Pointes in patients taking indapamide, it seems to have been generally well tolerated, and the current USPI does not note a risk of QT prolongation or TdP.
L2095: Insight concerning blockade of the I_{Ks} current is informed by patients with Long QT syndrome 1 (LQT1), a genetic condition where mutations in the channel responsible for I_{Ks} impair its
L2096: function. These patients have diminished shortening of the QT interval when the heart rate increases and are at increased risk of TdP during times of elevated heart rate and when recovering from elevated heart rate. Expert consensus recommendations for these patients include avoidance of QT prolonging medications, identification and correction of electrolyte abnormalities, and the use of beta blockers. While the first two recommendations were incorporated into the quizartinib development program, the use of beta-blocker therapy to treat acquired forms of Long QT syndrome is not standard. Based on the risk of greater QT interval prolongation with heart rate increases, we recommend a post-marketing requirement for an exercise test study in healthy volunteers and patients to evaluate the impact of heart rate on the cardiac safety of quizartinib. The patient study should include patients on beta-blockers to observe for mitigating effects on the incidence of quizartinib-related adverse reactions.
L2097: This preclinical evidence portended the increased rate of QTc prolonged AEs reported in the above safety analysis in both the newly diagnosed patients and in the overall ISS population. Indeed, dedicated response analysis of changes in QTcF during the continuation phase showed dose dependent increase in the change in QTcF during this phase of treatment. Two caveats to this analysis are that patients must have tolerated quizartinib through two prior phases of treatment to reach the continuation phase, selecting out those patients that could not tolerate quizartinib or had extreme QTcF responses to quizartinib. Secondly, dosing during this phase of treatment was dependent on the change of QTcF. Therefore, these results do not reflect the response that would be expected from a population randomly assigned to either dose level but rather what might be expected if dose escalation was dependent on change in QTcF and after disregarding patients that could not tolerate quizartinib treatment. The trend suggests diminishing elevation of QTcF for the marginal increase in dose, when this trend reflects the selection of patients to the higher dose that had less increase in QTcF change with the lower dose.
L2098: We agree with the Applicant that most subjects Study AC220-A-U302 were on at least one QTc prolonging medication at some time during the study. Fluconazole, ondansetron, levofloxacin and ciprofloxacin were used in more than 10% of subjects. The Applicant investigated this pharmacodynamic drug interaction on the QTc interval in their population exposure-response analysis and concluded that concomitant use of QTc prolonging drugs with quizartinib did not further increase the QTc interval. These negative findings are not persuasive because the analysis has many limitations (e.g., Applicant lumped all QTc prolonging drugs together without accounting for potency and dosage) and the sensitivity to detect an interaction was not demonstrated. Therefore, the effects of coadministration of QTc prolongation drugs that are IKr blockers with quizartinib have not been characterized.
L2099: ## **Risk of Torsade de Pointes and Cardiac Arrest**
L2100: As noted above, there were no reported cases of Torsade by PT analysis in Study AC220-A-U302 study. However, there were two cases of cardiac arrest on the quizartinib arm, one grade 4 and
L2101: one grade 5. There were no reported cases of cardiac arrest or Torsade de Pointes on the placebo arm.
L2102: The Grade 4 event occurred in 63 year old female patient (Subject ID (b) (6)) who developed ventricular fibrillation and cardiac arrest on day 5 of quizartinib treatment. The patient had personal history of Long QT syndrome diagnosed years prior to developing AML and had a pretreatment QTcF of 463. She was started at a reduced dose of quizartinib of 20 mg daily due to being on a strong CYP3A inhibitor Posaconazole (which is also a QT prolonging medication). During these days, the patient was also placed on standing furosemide, which induced severe hypokalemia to 2.4 by the time of the cardiac arrest. She was cardioverted and had resumption of spontaneous circulation. QTcF after the event was reported as 587 ms. Tracing of the VF were not available for FDA review. She was permanently discontinued from study treatment.
L2103: *FDA comment: This patient should have been excluded from the study due to her personal history of Long QT syndrome and pretreatment QTcF being above the 450ms cutoff. However, even with these risk factors, we see how concomitant QTc prolonging medications and severe iatrogenic hypokalemia increased the chances of this event.*
L2104: The Grade 5 event occurred in a 54 year old female patient (Subject (b) (6)) who developed cardiac arrest on day 79 of treatment. The subject was not in remission after a first cycle of induction and underwent a second induction cycle followed by quizartinib. It was noted during this second quizartinib cycle that treatment was interrupted on day 72 for prolonged QTc of 493 but resumed the next day (day 73) at a reduced dose of 30 mg (40-&gt;30mg). On study day 78 the patient was noted to have severe hypokalemia with a potassium of 2.6. Quizartinib dosing was not held and was continued with potassium supplementation (including IV potassium). The next day (day 79), the patient was reported in good clinical condition at 12:30 but had sudden cardiac arrest at 14:00. FDA review of the tracing from the event was consistent with Torsade de Pointes. The medical team was unable to restore normal rhythm, and the patient passed away. Other QTc prolonging medications the patient was taking at the time of the event included ciprofloxacin, linezolid, and piperacillin/tazobactam.
L2105: *FDA comment: Per the narrative, the patient had resolution of her initial QTc prolongation to a QTc 407 the day prior (day 78) to her cardiac arrest. This is despite reports that her serum potassium that day was severely low at 2.6.*
L2106: *The common features of these two cases include middle aged women, recovering from induction chemotherapy, exposure to several QTc prolonging medications, and severe hypokalemia. Avoiding QTc prolonging medications and severe hypokalemia appear to be two*
L2107: *factors that can be addressed to mitigate the clinical risk of ventricular arrhythmia and cardiac arrest.*
L2108: While the Applicant contends that the rate of QTc prolongation was manageable and asymptomatic, they do not satisfactorily address that 2/265 (0.8%) of quizartinib-treated patients had a cardiac arrest during the first phase of treatment. FDA and the Applicant both agree that additional factors including history of long QT syndrome, hypokalemia, and concomitant QTc prolonging medications contributed to these two events. However, FDA views this as disconcerting since it suggests that Study Investigators, despite protocol warnings and contraindications about the risk of quizartinib treatment, still enrolled and treated a patient with a history of Long QT syndrome, continued to prescribe concomitant QTc prolonging medications with quizartinib and prolonged QTc, and continued quizartinib treatment despite patients having severe hypokalemia (potassium levels &lt;3 \mu\text{M}). Moreover, these events are potentially fatal arrhythmias that should be prevented from occurring. Once a patient develops TdP, treatment is challenging even in the inpatient hospital setting. Also, as noted earlier, although *FLT3-ITD* mutant AML is serious life-threatening disease, the availability of an alternative FDA approved *FLT3* inhibitor for this indication and line of therapy necessitates that appropriate precaution be taken with the choice of patient and with respect to quizartinib-specific patient management. Lastly, these events occurred in younger patients during their initial treatment with intention to cure. This means that possibly preventable deaths that occur might be in patients that would otherwise be long-term survivors. Therefore, we conclude that active measures to ensure provider education of these factors, including absolute contraindications, temporary contraindications, unique mechanism of action, and patient monitoring guidelines are necessary for the safe and effective use of quizartinib outside the intensive monitoring of a clinical trial.
L2109: Lastly, in order to track the incidence of severe and fatal cardiac arrhythmias in patients treated with quizartinib, with its unique mechanism of QT interval prolongation, we recommend a post-marketing requirement for a prospective, observational study to further assess the risk.

L2123: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2124: In Study AC220-A-U302, 91 subjects (34.3%) in the quizartinib arm and 73 subjects (27.2%) in the placebo arm experienced hepatic TEAEs in the reviewed SMQs. Most of the events identified were non-serious and none resulted in a fatal outcome see (Table 108).
L2125: Treatment-emergent AEs and serious AEs in the quizartinib arm were mainly liver function test (LFT) abnormalities and had alternative/contributing factors (such as sepsis, baseline LFT abnormalities, concomitant hepatotoxic medication, and autoimmune hemolytic anemia), improved on continued study drug administration, or had no recurrence on rechallenge. No serious cases of hepatic failure or toxic hepatitis were observed in Study AC220-A-U302 (ISS Table 4.1.30).
L2126: The incidence of TESAEs of hepatic failure or drug-induced liver injury was low in the All AML Pool. The reported cases of drug-induced liver injury, hepatic failure, or acute hepatic failure had alternative etiologies and risk factors, including sepsis, cardiac failure, AML progression with liver involvement, and GVHD.
L2127: In Study AC220-A-U302, elevations of AT were mostly limited to \ge 3 to &lt;10 \times ULN and occurred in numerically higher percentages of subjects in the quizartinib arm than in the placebo arm. Elevations of TBL \ge 2 \times ULN occurred in similar percentages of subjects in both treatment arms. Similar percentages of subjects in the quizartinib and placebo arms had concurrent AT \ge 3 \times ULN with TBL \ge 2 \times ULN (see Table 107).
L2128: The LFT abnormalities in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool were consistent with those observed in Study AC220-A-U302 (see Table 107). Overall, subjects with concurrent AT \ge 3 \times ULN and TBL \ge 2 \times ULN had additional contributing factors identified, most frequently co-suspect concomitant medications, severe infection or sepsis, AML disease progression or GVHD, or had combination liver enzyme elevations that were mild and transient, resolved on continued study drug administration, or did not reoccur upon rechallenge. There were no
L2129: notable differences in findings across the dose groups. An evaluation of these subjects does not reveal a pattern of liver injury associated with quizartinib therapy.
L2130: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2131: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment. FDA independently screened (both broadly and narrowly) for Hy's law cases in the laboratory data from Study AC220-A-U302. We found that subjects that met criteria for screening had transient elevations of LFTs that resolved either without change in study drug dosing or with study drug interruption. No patients on the quizartinib arm of the study had to discontinue study drug due to elevated LFTs alone whereas one patient on the placebo arm discontinued study drug for hyperbilirubinemia.

The patient had worsening of cardiac function for several weeks after cessation of study drug. The case was complicated by previous anthracycline use, limited exposure to quizartinib, and continued worsening of cardiac function after cessation of quizartinib.
L2148: ## 8.2.5.4 Differentiation Syndrome and Acute Febrile Neutrophilic Dermatosis
L2149: Of the 1081 subjects treated with quizartinib in the All AML Pool, 2 subjects (0.2%; both in the &gt;60 mg group) had investigator-reported TEAEs of differentiation syndrome (DS) in the setting of R/R AML (Table 110).
L2150: Data from Study AC220-007 in subjects with R/R AML using the FDA criteria (Norsworthy, 2020) for DS identified 67 (27.8%) quizartinib-treated subjects and 34 of the 94 subjects (36.2%) treated with salvage chemotherapy. On Applicant medical review, no events were assessed as definite or probable DS in Study AC220-007. A total of 12 quizartinib-treated subjects (5%) were identified as having possible DS based on the clinical presentation, though a clear alternative etiology may have been present. None had a fatal outcome due to possible DS. No medical review of salvage chemotherapy cases was done.
L2151: No events of DS were reported in subjects with newly diagnosed AML from Study AC220-A-U302 (Table 110).
L2152: Using the same FDA's algorithmic approach for identification of potential cases of DS, 76 of the 265 subjects (28.7%) were identified from the AC220-A-U302 study dataset. Based on the Applicant review, all the events identified from the search were assessed as 'not a DS'. Of note, 79 of the 268 subjects (29.5%) in the placebo arm of Study AC220-A-U302 were also identified as having potential DS using the FDA's algorithm. Cases in placebo-treated subjects were not further evaluated.

L2156: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2157: Quizartinib can cause terminal differentiation of AML blast cells (Sexauer, 2012), which can potentially lead to the development of DS in subjects with R/R AML receiving quizartinib monotherapy.
L2158: DS is dependent on differentiation of blasts cells and as such is considered unlikely to occur in subjects with newly diagnosed AML receiving induction and consolidation chemotherapy, where rapid disappearance of myeloid blasts occurs. Data from the AC220-A-U302 study has not identified any occurrence of differentiation in quizartinib-treated subjects.
L2159: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2160: FDA reviewed the possible cases of DS identified from Study AC220-A-U302 and agreed that they had alternative etiologies that were explained by the clinical context (culture positive infections, neutropenic fever responding to empiric antibiotics, etc.) and responded quickly to alternative treatments or were confirmed to be from alternative causes. FDA notes that in the R/R setting, there were cases of DS identified by investigators and by screening of Study AC220-007 for possible DS cases (12 possible cases noted above). Moreover, there were 20 cases of acute febrile neutrophilic dermatosis identified in the ISS population but not within Study AC220-A-U302.
L2161: Of note, FDA disagrees with the Applicant that there were no fatal cases of DS on Study AC220-007. FDA adjudicated 3 cases of possible DS that contributed to the patients' death and these cases are detailed below.
L2162: - USUBJID (b) (6) was a 62-year-old male who met Montesinos criteria on D1 (on 30 mg quizartinib) when he presented with G1 febrile neutropenia and then G1
L2163: hypotension on D4, with no stop date. Initially treated with antibiotics, he subsequently presented with neutropenic sepsis on D9 which was also treated with antibiotics; a blood culture was positive for e. faecium on D8 supporting an infectious etiology for these symptoms, but the neutropenic sepsis worsened on D23, when he developed multiple skin lesions which were erythematous and painful. Of note a CT on D20 showed "diffuse swelling of the subcutaneous tissues and platysma muscle on the left side of the jaw but no discrete collection or mass lesion, which could mean inflammatory or infectious process." On D24 a CXR showed fluid overload and the patient developed hypoxemia, treated with diuretics. On D25 his PICC line was removed as it was thought to be the source of the skin lesions. Quizartinib was interrupted on D28 due to sepsis, but it was restarted on D37 at a reduced dose (20 mg). On D46 the patient was noted to be hemodynamically stable with improving inflammatory markers, and quizartinib was reescalated to 30 mg; the patient subsequently deteriorated (unknown date) with sepsis, arm pain, swelling, and an MRI showing myositis of the right abductor muscles, as well as fast AFib with refractory blood pressure, noted in the narrative to be due to electrolyte abnormality. The quizartinib was discontinued on D55, and on D64 the patient died of neutropenic sepsis. The patient's WBC count decreased from &gt;293000 at screening to 500 at the end of treatment, the blast count decreased from &gt;287000 at screening to undetectable at the end of treatment, and the ANC peaked at 320 on D45, without much change throughout the treatment course. Also, on D30 the BM showed no blasts, but a note showed that "there are a number of nucleated cells in the trails which are mainly neutrophils." The patient was treated with antibiotics and supportive care, but no steroids.
L2164: Reviewer comment: Although it is possible that all of the symptoms above were due to infection, the combination of fever, fluid overload, myositis and hypotension in the setting of disappearance of blasts and a BM with predominantly neutrophils, that improved with quizartinib interruption and dose reduction, and rapidly deteriorated with dose re-escalation, and was not treated with steroids, must be considered at least possibly a case on the DS/AFND spectrum due to the quizartinib. The restarting and dose-escalation of quizartinib and lack of steroid administration in this case highlight the fact that an event on this spectrum was not in the differential diagnosis for practitioners treating patients with quizartinib.
L2165: - USUBJID (b) (6) was a 31-year-old female who presented with respiratory distress/dyspnea and fever on D42 of quizartinib (30 mg), with bilateral pleural effusions and peripheral edema. Associated WBC was 21930, no report of whether or not there were blasts, although per the narrative quizartinib was discontinued on this day due to PD (no data from ADBL dataset post D29). She was treated with antibiotics and supportive care, no steroids were administered. She died on D44 due to G5 respiratory distress. Notably, she had been hospitalized from D19 onward with febrile neutropenia and staph bacteremia treated with antibiotics and supportive care; these resolved and she developed G3 lung infection, suspected to be fungal on D23, which improved on D37, and her dyspnea
L2166: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L2167: associated with this resolved on D39 as well. Of note, quizartinib had been decreased to 20 mg on D19 due to QT prolongation, and was increased back to 30 mg on D41, 1 day prior to development of the ultimately fatal respiratory distress and 3 days prior to death.
L2168: Reviewer comment: The proximity of the increased quizartinib dose to her symptoms, the resolution of the previous bacterial infection, the rapidity of progression of symptoms in the context of pleural effusions and peripheral edema and the lack of administration of steroids, all without evidence of a rapid increase in blast count, raise the concern for DS as a COD in this patient.
L2169: - USUBJID (b) (6) was a 47-year-old female who presented on D22 of quizartinib therapy (90mg) with a rash and subsequent pyrexia, general aches and right eye swelling; the rash was diagnosed as AFND (no biopsy mentioned). The patient had associated dyspnea, and a CT showed “chest infection”; a bronchoscopy was positive for rhino- and enterovirus on PCR. WBC was 1100 and ANC was 800, and remained between 700-800 throughout the course. Treatment included antibiotics and steroids. Quizartinib was interrupted on D29; pyrexia and AFND resolved on D37 and 42, respectively, and quizartinib was restarted at 60 mg daily on D43 and interrupted again on D71 due to an “arm wound.” She presented again on D78 with AFND and pyrexia with associated edema, feeling “unwell” and diarrhea; per the narrative she was “noted to have...sepsis” and dyspnea. WBC was 1700 with an ANC of 600, no blasts. CXR showed “widespread consolidation” and per the narrative she was diagnosed with a fungal infection and parainfluenza. Quizartinib was permanently discontinued due to these events. Treatment included antibiotics and steroids. All events were ongoing at the time of death on D89, which was assessed as due to progressive AML. It is noted that no labs were included in the ADLB dataset beyond D69, BMA had 0% blasts at C2D1(D29), and no peripheral blasts were recorded in that dataset beyond D8 (0.24).
L2170: Reviewer comment: The associated dyspnea, pyrexia and AFND in the setting of decreasing blasts raise the possibility that this is a fatal event on the DS/AFND spectrum.
L2171: FDA agrees with Applicant's supposition that DS is unlikely to occur within the treatment regimen under consideration for newly diagnosed patients. This is likely due to debulking effect of induction chemotherapy and the relative resistance of AML cells remaining after induction chemotherapy to undergo differentiation from a 14-day course of quizartinib. FDA included DS and AFND in labeling since these were serious events that might occur if quizartinib is used outside of the indicated population. Labeling noted that these occurred within development program but in a different clinical setting.
L2172: ## 8.2.6. Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability
L2173: Data:
L2174: Refer to Section 8.1.2 for PRO results.
L2175: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2176: Notwithstanding the caveats with respect to interpretation of the data discussed previously, the subscales relevant for safety and tolerability included are fatigue and nausea/vomiting, where improvement was noticeable in the Continuation Phase for both subscales. The results for fatigue and nausea/vomiting suggest no detrimental impact from addition of quizartinib to standard-of-care chemotherapy for newly diagnosed *FLT3-ITD (+) AML*.
L2177: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2178: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment that both nausea/vomiting and fatigue as components of PRO results were improved from baseline. However, we note that the results suggested greater PRO scale improvement with placebo than with quizartinib. Interestingly, this difference is reflected in the higher rate of nausea (23% quizartinib vs. 9% placebo) and vomiting (15% quizartinib vs. 8%) reported as TEAEs in quizartinib-treated patients during the continuation phase than in the placebo treated patients (Table 95). However, fatigue reported as a TEAE during this phase showed no difference (13% quizartinib vs. 13% placebo). This suggests that PRO data might better capture patient fatigue than fatigue reported as a TEAE.
L2179: ## 8.2.7. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L2180: ## **Data:**
L2181: Subgroup analyses of TEAEs by intrinsic factors (eg, age, sex, race, and ethnicity) and extrinsic factors (eg, geographic region and concomitant medications) were performed for Study AC220-A-U302 and the All AML Pool.
L2182: The analyses according to race, ethnicity, and geographic region were hampered by the limited number of subjects in some of the subgroups (Black/African American, Hispanic/Latino), but in general, the type and incidence of the TEAEs most frequently reported across the overall Safety Analysis Set (gastrointestinal disorders, infections, pyrexia, cytopenias, skin disorders, hypokalemia, ALT increased, and ECG QT prolonged) were similar in the different subgroups (ISS Table 4.1.15).
L2183: There were some notable findings in subgroup analyses observed for QT interval prolongation, with the TEAE of QT prolongation reported more frequently in females compared with males, higher percentages of females than males who had QTcF &gt;450 ms and QTcF &gt;480 ms, and increases in QTcF &gt;30 ms or &gt;60 ms in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302. However, no differences in QTcF &gt;500 ms were observed between males and females in either treatment arm (Table 10.27, CSR AC220-A-U302, eCTD 5.3.5.1).
L2184: Similarly, concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitor was shown to be associated with a higher incidence of QTcF prolongation on ECG in Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool.
L2185: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L2186: In contrast, age appeared not to be a factor impacting the incidence of QT interval prolongation based on reported TEAE or ECG data, with no clear trend for increased incidence with increased age in Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to 60 mg dose group of the All AML Pool (ISS Table 7.1.2).
L2187: A subgroup analysis by use of QT-prolonging medication and by duration of treatment showed that concomitant administration of QT-prolonging medications had no significant impact on the incidence of QTcF prolongation (Module 2.7.4, Section 4.2). The incidence of QTcF prolongation (&gt;450 ms and change from baseline &gt;60 ms) appeared to increase with longer treatment duration, although this is likely a feature of event accrual during the longer follow-up time in subjects with longer treatment duration.
L2188: As summarized in Section 19.4.3.4, the C-QTcF E-R analysis showed that age, daunorubicin, hypokalemia, and calcium levels but not sex, body weight, or concomitant QT-prolonging medications were as identified as significant covariates.
L2189: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2190: Overall, the safety data with quizartinib were generally similar across different age, sex, and concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitor subgroups in Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to 60 mg dose group of the All AML Pool. With the limitations outlined above, the type and incidence of the TEAEs most frequently reported were generally similar in the different subgroups of race, ethnicity, and geographic region.
L2191: Although some numerical differences in the incidence of some categories of TEAEs (eg, TESAEs, TEAEs associated with death as outcome, and TEAEs associated with study drug discontinuation or interruption) were observed these were assessed as not indicative of clinically meaningful differences in the safety profile of quizartinib.
L2192: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2193: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position. The FDA notes that multiple studies have indicated that females are more susceptible to acquired long QT syndrome.
L2194: ## 8.2.8. **Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials**
L2195: ## Data:
L2196: No specific safety studies are included in the NDA.
L2197: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2198: Not applicable.
L2199: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2200: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment.
L2201: ## **8.2.9. Additional Safety Explorations**
L2202: ## **Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development**
L2203: ## **Data:**
L2204: No human carcinogenicity studies have been conducted.
L2205: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2206: In Study AC220-A-U302, 7 subjects (2.6%) in the quizartinib arm and 8 subjects (3.0%) in the placebo arm experienced a TEAE in the MedDRA SOC Neoplasms benign, malignant and unspecified. No individual PT was reported by &gt;1 subject in the quizartinib arm. No specific trend could be identified in the nature of the neoplastic events reported from the study, and based on the available information, no evidence of causal association with quizartinib could be identified (ISS Table 4.1.3).
L2207: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2208: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment. Events were balanced between arms and without clear trend to occurrence.
L2209: ## **Human Reproduction and Pregnancy**
L2210: ## **Data:**
L2211: Women of childbearing potential were required to have a negative serum pregnancy test upon entry into Study AC220-A-U302 and required to use highly effective birth control as defined in the clinical study protocol upon enrollment, during the Treatment Period and for 6 months following the last dose of study drug. Men were required to be surgically sterile or willing to use highly effective birth control as defined in the clinical study protocol upon enrollment, during the Treatment Period, and for 6 months following the last dose of study drug. There was 1 case of pregnancy in the placebo arm of Study AC220-A-U302 and no lactation-related events were reported in the quizartinib development program (see Table 21: Subject Disposition in Study AC220-A-U302).
L2212: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2213: In an embryo-fetal development study in rats, fetotoxicity (lower fetal weights and effects on skeletal ossification) and teratogenicity (high incidence of fetal malformations) were observed. However, no clinical studies have been conducted that assess the use of quizartinib during
L2214: pregnancy or breastfeeding. While one case of pregnancy was reported in Study AC220-A-U302, this case occurred on the placebo arm; no pregnancies have been reported in quizartinib-treated subjects in the clinical development program.
L2215: There are no human data on the effect of quizartinib on fertility. Based on findings in animals, female and male fertility may be impaired with treatment with quizartinib.
L2216: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2217: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment that embryo-fetal toxicity was observed in animal studies and is a potential risk to humans. Therefore, embryo-fetal toxicity has been included in warnings and precautions section of the USPI. Lastly, impaired fertility was observed in animal studies and this information was also included in the USPI.
L2218: ## **Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth**
L2219: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2220: Quizartinib is currently being investigated in the Phase 1/2 pediatric study, AC220-A-U202, in R/R *FLT3*-ITD (+)AML.
L2221: Study AC220-A-U202 (NCT03793478) is ongoing to evaluate the safety, PK, PD, and efficacy of quizartinib in combination with fludarabine and cytarabine (FLA) chemotherapy for re-induction chemotherapy, and as a single agent continuation therapy in pediatric R/R *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML subjects aged \ge 1 month old to 18 years old (and young adults up to 21 years old). As of September 2022, 9 subjects (aged between 3 and 17 years old) have been treated in the Phase 1 dose-escalation cohort with quizartinib 40 mg/m2 if body surface area (BSA) &lt;1.5 m2, or 60 mg if BSA \ge 1.5 m2. There were no deaths during treatment; 4 subjects died during the long-term follow-up (3 due to disease progression and 1 due to GVHD after bone marrow transplant). A DLT occurred in 1 subject: delayed ANC and platelet recovery. TESAEs were reported by 7 subjects; none of the reported TESAE were fatal. Apart from SAEs of sepsis and febrile neutropenia, which were both reported by 2 subjects each, the remaining events were reported by single subjects and included: empyema, streptococcal sepsis, bilirubin conjugated increased, sinus tachycardia, seizure, dyspnea, hypoxia, pulmonary edema, distributive shock, herpes zoster reactivation, and anal fissure. Two subjects discontinued study treatment because of an AE (ECG QT prolonged and thrombocytopenia). The most common Grade \ge 3 TEAEs were blood cytopenias. No subjects experienced QTcF &gt;500 ms, and 1 subject had QTcF &gt;480 ms.
L2222: No new safety signals have been detected and the safety profile is consistent with the known safety profile from quizartinib experience in adult subjects.
L2223: Safety and PK continue to be evaluated to determine the recommended Phase 2 for the expansion cohort (\ge 1 to \le 21 years) and the dose-escalation cohort for the younger age cohort (\ge 1 month to &lt;1 year old).
L2224: The effects of quizartinib on growth have not been assessed and currently remains unknown.
L2225: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2226: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment. Study AC220-A-U302 is ongoing. The effects of quizartinib on growth have not been assessed. Safety results in the 9 subjects treated to date are comparable to the those observed in adult studies.
L2227: ## **Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound**
L2228: ## **Data:**
L2229: Cumulatively, there have been 3 reports of accidental overdose in the All AML Pool, which were reported as SAEs (ISS Table 4.1.11). Symptoms associated with overdose included vomiting, but there were no effects on QTcF interval duration.
L2230: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2231: No studies have been conducted that assess drug overdose, and there is limited clinical experience with quizartinib overdose. Repeated doses up to 450 mg have been clinically evaluated in subjects, and the DLT was prolongation of the QT interval. There are no specific antidotes to quizartinib. For substantial overdose, supportive measures should be provided as necessary, with the evaluation of hematology and ECG monitoring as well as attention to serum electrolytes and concomitant medications that may predispose to QT interval prolongation and/or TdP. Patients should be managed with symptomatic and supportive care.
L2232: No studies have been conducted that assess drug abuse. However, given the nature of quizartinib, drug abuse is not anticipated. No studies have been conducted that assess withdrawal or rebound effects of quizartinib. However, given the target patient population and the nature of quizartinib, no such effects are anticipated.
L2233: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2234: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment. While higher exposure to the drug would be expected to induce higher rates of adverse events, the fact that the development program tested doses 10-fold greater than the proposed dose in the induction and consolidation setting provides a measure of assurance that accidental overdose within this range of exposure would at least be tolerated temporarily. We agree that supportive care and attention to electrolyte and ECG monitoring would be appropriate based on recommended labeling in general. Lastly, we concur that given the mechanism of action, adverse event profile, and the lack of euphoric effects from use that quizartinib abuse is not anticipated.
L2235: ## 8.2.10. Safety in the Postmarket Setting
L2236: ## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L2237: 326
L2238: *Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)*
L2239: *Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.*
L2240: ## Data:
L2241: Quizartinib was approved by the MHLW in Japan in June 2019 for the treatment of patients with R/R *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML, as detected by an MHLW-approved assay based on the improved response rates and OS in R/R *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML subjects in a randomized Phase 3 study, referred to as Study AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R; Cortes, 2019).
L2242: Cumulatively as of 28 Oct 2021, an estimated 228 patients have received quizartinib in the postmarketing setting, all of whom were in Japan. The safety profile of quizartinib in the postmarketing setting is consistent with the safety profile as established in the development program, and no new safety concerns have been identified. Reported SAEs were mostly events related to AML disease progression and cytopenias (neutrophil count decreased, and febrile neutropenia). Events related to AML disease progression were also the most common events associated with fatal outcome. Reported AEs of ECG QT prolonged were mostly non-serious and not associated with any ventricular arrhythmia events (eCTD 2.7.4, Section 8).
L2243: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2244: Review of post marketing safety data did not reveal any new safety concerns and the reported events were generally consistent with the safety profile established in the quizartinib development program as summarized above.
L2245: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2246: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of the post marketing safety data from to date.
L2247: ## **Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting**
L2248: ## Data:
L2249: The safety and tolerability profile of quizartinib has been adequately characterized for use in the intended population in Study AC220-A-U302. The safety profile seen is also consistent with that observed throughout the quizartinib clinical development program as evidenced by data for the 30 to 60 mg dose level in the All AML Pool. QT interval prolongation and the associated potential risk of ventricular arrhythmias and blood cytopenias and infections are the main safety risks for quizartinib in patients with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML. The risk of QT prolongation/ventricular arrhythmia has been adequately managed with the risk mitigation procedures implemented in Study AC220-A-U302, which comprise ECG monitoring for QT prolongation, QTcF-based quizartinib dose interruption and reduction, and correction of additional risk factors (eg, monitoring for and correction of electrolyte abnormalities, quizartinib dose modification for concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor use) and more frequent monitoring of ECG during concomitant therapy with known QT prolongation.
L2250: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2251: Management of myelosuppression and associated risk of infections and bleeding is part of the routine clinical practice for the treatment of patients with newly diagnosed AML. In the study, events associated with myelosuppression could be appropriately managed by transfusion and growth factor support, as well as through protocol-specified study drug dose modifications. Overall, the majority of TEAEs of blood cytopenias were managed with study drug interruptions or dose reductions, and few subjects discontinued quizartinib due to these events.
L2252: These measures are expected to adequately mitigate the risks conveyed in the proposed label and are readily implemented within standard medical practice for the treatment of newly diagnosed AML. The risk minimization measures for the risk of QT prolongation/ventricular arrhythmias in the quizartinib label will be supplemented by additional communication to prescribers and patients/caregivers.
L2253: Safety risks beyond those included in the proposed draft label are not anticipated. The risks of DDIs with strong CYP3A inhibitors, strong and moderate CYP3A inducers, embryo-fetal toxicity, and female and male fertility are also adequately conveyed in the proposed labeling.
L2254: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2255: FDA does not fully agree with the Applicant's assessment. While the FDA does anticipate that the most common AEs related to GI toxicity, cytopenia, and infections are standard in the clinical setting of acute leukemia, the FDA has concluded that additional measures must be implemented to further minimize the risk of ventricular arrhythmias in quizartinib-treated patients. The Applicant has proposed a communication plan REMS to assist with provider and patient education concerning this risk. However, the FDA clinical team concluded that additional active and required education measures should be implemented to ensure the safe use of quizartinib in clinical practice (i.e., REMS with Elements to Assure Safe Use [ETASU]).
L2256: ## 8.2.11. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L2257: ## Data:
L2258: See data in Sections 8.2.4 and 8.2.5.
L2259: In the pivotal study, in both treatment arms, more than half of the deaths during the study were attributed to AML disease progression or to AEs that were considered secondary to AML. More early deaths (ie, deaths within 60 days of the initiation of study drug) occurred with quizartinib compared with placebo. In total, 20 subjects (7.5%) and 13 subjects (4.9%), respectively died within 60 days of the study drug initiation, of which 15 subjects (5.7%) and 9 subjects (3.4%), respectively, died within 30 days of study drug initiation. In both treatment arms, the majority of early deaths were due to AEs (Table 72).
L2260: Treatment-emergent AEs associated with death as outcome occurred in 30 subjects (11.3%) in the quizartinib arm and 26 subjects (9.7%) in the placebo arm during the overall study period. In both treatment arms, the most frequently reported TEAEs with an outcome of death
L2261: (including early deaths) were due to infections, which occurred mainly in the Induction and Consolidation Phases and were more common in the quizartinib arm (Table 74). Infections are a known risk for subjects with AML receiving induction and consolidation chemotherapy.
L2262: More subjects in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 experienced TEAEs associated with study drug discontinuation compared with placebo (54 subjects [20.4%] and 23 subjects [8.6%], respectively). The most frequently reported TEAEs associated with discontinuation of quizartinib were infections (septic shock, sepsis, and pneumonia) and cytopenias (thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and febrile neutropenia). In the placebo arm, the most common TEAE associated with discontinuation were also infections (pneumonia).
L2263: The most frequently reported TEAEs in both treatment arms of Study AC220-A-U302 were gastrointestinal disorders (diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting), infections and infestations (pneumonia and sepsis), general disorders and administration site conditions (pyrexia, oedema peripheral, and fatigue), and blood and lymphatic system disorders (febrile neutropenia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, and anemia).
L2264: Among the most commonly reported TEAEs (\ge 20\%), the events of nausea, vomiting, febrile neutropenia, neutropenia, and headache, occurred more frequently (in \ge 2\% more subjects) in the quizartinib arm than in the placebo arm.
L2265: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2266: Safety data from Study AC220-A-U302 were consistent with the safety profile of quizartinib as established over the prior development program and no new safety concerns were identified. The most common types of TEAEs are those commonly seen in subjects with newly diagnosed AML receiving standard intensive chemotherapy and generally occurred at comparable rates in the quizartinib and placebo arms. Blood cytopenias (neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, and anemia) and infections were the most common severe and serious TEAEs and the most common categories of events associated with quizartinib discontinuation, interruption, and dose reduction.
L2267: The incidence of TEAEs associated with fatal outcome was low, and the most common fatal TEAEs (including those occurring within 30 and 60 days of the start of quizartinib) were infections, which are a known risk for subjects with AML receiving induction and consolidation chemotherapy.
L2268: There was no clinically relevant variability of the safety profile across subpopulations and no evidence of increasing toxicity with long-term treatment with quizartinib for up to 36 cycles (3 years). No new event types were observed among subjects receiving quizartinib &gt;12 cycles (12 months), and the most frequently reported TEAEs occurred at similar incidence rates regardless of length of treatment duration, with the exception of blood disorders, which increased with longer treatment duration. Quizartinib was not associated with clinically apparent drug-induced liver injury.
L2269: Quizartinib causes QT interval prolongation in a dose-dependent and exposure-dependent manner, and QT interval prolongation on ECG was commonly observed in Study AC220-A-U302. However, most of the QT prolongation events were of mild or moderate severity and were manageable by quizartinib dose modification and correction of additional risk factors (eg, monitoring for and correction of electrolyte abnormalities, quizartinib dose modification for QTcF prolongation, and for concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor use) and more frequent monitoring of ECG during concomitant therapy with known QT prolongation. QT prolongation is a risk factor for the occurrence of ventricular arrhythmias, including TdP, which were observed at very low incidence at the recommended doses of quizartinib and generally occurred in subjects with other significant risk factors for cardiac arrhythmia. This further demonstrates that the clinical impact of QT prolongation can be appropriately managed with QTc-based dose modifications, correction of electrolyte abnormalities, and dose adjustments for concomitant use with strong CYP3A inhibitors.
L2270: As a substrate of CYP3A, quizartinib should not be administered concomitantly with strong or moderate CYP3A inducers.
L2271: Although no data in humans are available that assess the use of quizartinib during pregnancy or breastfeeding or its effect on fertility, nonclinical safety data indicate that quizartinib can be associated with fetotoxicity and teratogenicity if administered during pregnancy and that impaired fertility could occur in treated patients.
L2272: Overall, QT interval prolongation and the associated potential risk of ventricular arrhythmias and blood cytopenias and infections are the main safety risks for quizartinib in subjects with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML. Results from Study AC220-A-U302 have demonstrated an acceptable safety and tolerability profile of quizartinib in the target population when given in combination with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy and then continued as monotherapy for up to 36 cycles with the proposed dosing regimen.
L2273: The safety data across the quizartinib development program in subjects with AML are consistent with the findings from the pivotal study AC220-A-U302. Apart from DS, which is considered a potential risk for quizartinib monotherapy for the treatment of patients with R/R AML, but is not anticipated to occur in patients with newly diagnosed AML receiving quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy, the safety profile of quizartinib is consistent across different AML indications and patient populations. Similarly, available post marketing safety data for quizartinib in patients with R/R AML have not revealed any additional safety concerns.
L2274: The safety results and efficacy observed in Study AC220-A-U302 establish that quizartinib is an effective and well-tolerated treatment option when combined with standard chemotherapy then as continuation monotherapy, for adults with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML. Therefore, the Applicant concludes that the data from Study AC220-A-U302 support a positive overall benefit-risk profile for quizartinib for the proposed indication.
L2275: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2276: FDA analysis of safety event rates differed with the Applicant's because of the use of FDA derived group terms as detailed in the Table 94 above. FDA also disagreed with Applicant's root cause of 27 deaths that occurred within 30 days of treatment as noted above. The overall safety profile for quizartinib based on FDA analysis of clinical trials is summarized in Table 111 below.
L2277: **Table 111: Safety summary of the pivotal study AC220-A-U302 and quizartinib-treated AML patients from the ISS**
L2278: AC220-A-U302	ISS AML
Quizartinib (N=265)	Placebo (N=268)	Quizartinib
N = 1081
Total Deaths	130 (49%)	156 (58%)	816 (75%)
30-day Mortality	15 (5.7%)	9 (3.4%)	52 (4.8%)
60-day Mortality	20 (7.5%)	13 (4.9%)	124 (11.5%)
On-treatment deaths*	32 (12%)	25 (9%)	311 (29%)
Grade 5 TEAEs	30 (11%)	26 (10%)	255 (24%)
All-Grade TEAEs	264 (100%)	265 (99%)	1073 (99%)
Grade 3-5 TEAEs	244 (92%)	240 (90%)	965 (89%)
Serious TEAEs (SAEs)	143 (54%)	123 (46%)	725 (67%)

Source: FDA analysis using adae.xpt, adsl.xpt
L2279: Total deaths were higher on the placebo arm than the quizartinib arm, which is reflective of the OS analysis. On-treatment deaths and grade 5 TEAEs were similar between arms but slightly
L2280: higher on the quizartinib arm, suggesting increased toxicity with quizartinib treatment. Increased toxicity is also reflected in the higher rate of TEAEs with discontinuation and serious TEAEs seen on the quizartinib arm. However, the rate of all-grade TEAEs and Grade 3-5 TEAEs (90% vs 92%) was essentially the same between arms. The increase in toxicity over the placebo arm is not unexpected for an add-on trial design where the backbone therapy is known to carry significant toxicity.
L2281: In the ISS AML population, total deaths, on-treatment deaths, Grade 5 TEAEs, and serious TEAEs were higher, likely reflecting the higher acuity of the R/R AML patients; although, higher quizartinib exposure could have contributed. The rate of TEAEs with discontinuation, however, was similar.
L2282: The safety profile of the most common TEAEs in Study AC220-A-U302 were generally consistent with those that occur in AML patients undergoing intensive induction and consolidation chemotherapy treatment. It is notable that only a subset of the most frequently occurring TEAEs were increased on the quizartinib arm with others being the same between both arms or higher on the placebo arm. The general category for these common TEAEs increased over placebo were GI toxicity, cytopenias, and infections. It is important to note, as seen by the results of the placebo arm, that these events are common in the clinical context and clinicians that treat AML are experienced with their general management.
L2283: Analysis of the safety profile of quizartinib during the maintenance phase of treatment allowed a comparison between quizartinib and placebo without the high background rate of TEAEs that occurred with induction and consolidation chemotherapy. As with the first two phases of treatment, the most common TEAEs that were increased with quizartinib treatment over placebo were cytopenias, infections, and GI toxicity. Interestingly, when these common TEAEs were further assessed in patient based on their transplant status, we saw that patients that had undergone HSCT during consolidation had higher rates of cytopenias and infection TEAEs during the maintenance phase than patients that had only undergone chemotherapy consolidation. This showed that maintenance therapy was less well tolerated post-HSCT consolidation than in patients that had only undergone chemotherapy consolidation.
L2284: The toxicity that occurs with quizartinib treatment that is not common in AML or from standard treatment is QT prolongation and risk of ventricular arrhythmias. Indeed, 2/265 (0.8%) of quizartinib treated patients had a cardiac arrest during the first phase of treatment on Study AC220-A-U302. While the Applicant notes that rate of QT prolongation seems manageable compared to other QT prolonging medications, the risk of cardiac arrest is not accurately reflected from this rate. FDA has concluded that the novel mechanism of QT prolongation increases the risk of cardiac arrest beyond the risk that would be otherwise suggested by more traditional QT prolonging medications (I_{Kr} inhibitors). This might be due to concomitant QT prolonging medications synergizing with quizartinib or to baseline QTc changes incompletely capturing the risk of ventricular arrhythmia for I_{Ks} inhibitors. Therefore, FDA concludes that
L2285: additional risk mitigations measures that ensure clinician education of these unique aspects of quizartinib mechanism of QT prolongation and mitigation measures are required for safe use in clinical practice.
L2286: Therefore, to mitigate the risks of serious and fatal cardiac toxicities, we recommend a REMS with ETASU to 1) ensure prescribers are able to identify the unique QT prolonging mechanism of quizartinib, 2) sure prescribers are able to identify the risk factors that are associated with Torsades de Pointes and cardiac arrest with quizartinib, and 3) ensure prescribers are able to identify the importance of providing risk mitigation measures including QTc interval monitoring, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, avoidance of concomitant QTc prolonging medications, and dose modifications/dose interruptions when indicated. With these additional safety measures, FDA agrees with the Applicant that based on the totality of efficacy and safety data, the benefit/risk profile of quizartinib in the treatment of patients with newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD AML is favorable.
L2287: ## SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L2288: ## 8.3. Statistical Issues
L2289: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2290: QuANTUM-First demonstrated a statistically significant effect on OS, with HR 0.78 (95% CI: 0.62, 0.98). No improvement was demonstrated for the key secondary endpoint of EFS. Of note, the EFS results are sensitive to choice of window for induction failure (defined per protocol as failure to achieve CR within 42 days of the start of the last cycle of induction chemotherapy). Exploratory analyses suggest that long duration of CR is observed with quizartinib (median duration of CR 38.6 months).
L2291: Based on our current analyses of data from QuANTUM-First, we note that patients may not benefit from quizartinib continuation/maintenance therapy post-HSCT. In an exploratory subgroup analysis of the 89/208 (43%) of patients who received maintenance therapy with VANFLYTA or placebo following consolidation chemotherapy, the OS HR was 0.40 (95% CI: 0.19, 0.84). Of 119/208 (57%) of patients who received maintenance therapy with VANFLYTA or placebo following HSCT, the OS HR was 1.62 (95% CI: 0.62, 4.22). For RFS, the corresponding hazard ratios were 0.93 (95% CI: 2.00) and 0.71 (95% CI: 0.35, 1.43). As the unadjusted analyses do not account for the unbalance in post-randomization variables and/or baseline characteristics between treatment arms among patients who entered Continuation, additional analyses attempted to account for such features. Results from these adjusted analyses were similar. Thus, these analyses suggest inconclusive evidence regarding use of quizartinib as continuation/maintenance therapy after HSCT.
L2292: While QuANTUM-First demonstrated a statistically significant effect on OS, questions remain about which patients in the intended population have a positive benefit-risk. Early OS (&lt;6 months) was lower in the quizartinib arm due in part to increased infections. No single subgroup appears to be driving this effect. Likewise, it is unclear whether quizartinib continuation provides any benefit to patients who receive HSCT. Though this conclusion is based on exploratory post-hoc analyses, a more formal analysis is not possible since QuANTUM-First was inadequately designed to isolate the effect of continuation therapy. To date, no FLT3 TKI has definitively demonstrated improved efficacy for continued treatment following HSCT (Levis, 2019).
L2293: ## 8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
L2294: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2295: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
## {VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
L2296: The review team was able to verify or clarify the efficacy and safety endpoints as provided by the Applicant. Substantial evidence of effectiveness was established based on improvement in overall survival demonstrated on the quizartinib treatment arm of the adequate and well controlled clinical Study AC220-A-U302 and on the supporting evidence provided by Study AC220-007 demonstrating achievement of CR+CRh with quizartinib monotherapy in the R/R setting. The review team noted that while maintenance appeared to benefit patients who had not undergone HSCT, those that had undergone HSCT did not benefit from quizartinib maintenance. Ultimately, there was inadequate demonstration of favorable benefit-risk for quizartinib maintenance post-HSCT. See Sections 1 and 8 for more information. This led to the limitations of use statement that quizartinib is not indicated as maintenance monotherapy following allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplant as improvement in OS with quizartinib in this setting has not been demonstrated. Lastly, the review team did not find the original management plan proposed by the Applicant sufficient to mitigate quizartinib-induced QTc prolongation and the risk of fatal cardiac arrhythmia. The review team asked the Applicant to develop an adequate REMS program to mitigate these risks, which the Applicant assented to at the end of the review cycle. Review of the REMS program follows below in section 12.

L2305: ## 9 Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L2306: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2307: Meetings with two separate Special Government Employees (SGEs) were conducted on February 27, 2023 and March 6, 2023. Noting the lack of statistical significance of the secondary efficacy endpoints of EFS and CR rate on QuANTUM-First, the lack of an OS benefit in patients treated with quizartinib maintenance post-HSCT, and the unique risk of QTc prolongation, FDA requested a discussion of benefit-risk for the proposed indication, as well as the need for additional education and risk mitigation measures to prevent serious and potentially fatal arrhythmias. The Agency requested that the SGE comment on the following:
L2308: - Whether quizartinib has a favorable benefit-risk profile for its proposed indication "...in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy, and as continuation monotherapy following consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 internal tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) positive as detected by an FDA-approved test."
- If the SGE concludes that the benefit-risk profile is favorable, the Agency requests commentary on the following items:Whether the benefit-risk profile is favorable for quizartinib continuation monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy and/or HSCT.
- Whether additional education and risk mitigation measures are needed (e.g., communication plan, certification of healthcare providers and pharmacists, etc.), in addition to a boxed warning, to convey information and management guidelines on quizartinib's unique, serious, and potentially fatal risk of arrhythmias.
L2309: The two SGEs concluded that quizartinib has a favorable benefit-risk for use in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy. Both SGEs, however, noted concerns with the benefit of post-HSCT maintenance therapy with quizartinib. SGE #1 expressed concerns regarding isolation of the impact of maintenance without having randomization just prior to initiation of maintenance. They conveyed that there is no evidence to support the efficacy of post-HSCT maintenance and only weak evidence of benefit post-consolidation chemotherapy in the absence of HSCT. They thought, however, that physicians would likely use the product for maintenance in patients tolerating the drug in the absence of HSCT. SGE #2 noted an unlikely benefit of maintenance therapy in the post-HSCT setting, but that without HSCT, patients appeared to benefit from post-consolidation therapy. They noted reservations with over-interpretation of the subgroup
L2310: data, which may lead physicians to prescribe quizartinib the way it was administered on the study. They encouraged conveying the additional data on the impact of maintenance to physicians so that they could justify variations from how the study was conducted.
L2311: Regarding the unique, serious and potentially fatal risk of arrhythmias, the two SGEs concluded that the risk demonstrated in the trial and the unique mechanism of QT prolongation necessitated measures beyond labeling to effectively educate the relevant providers. SGE#1 noted that with number of drugs now reported to prolong the QT interval, that physicians have become blasé about QT prolongation and would not appreciate the additional risk incurred by blockade of the slow potassium channel. SGE#2 also noted that required education that clearly conveys the unique and serious risks of blockade of this alternative potassium channel would be critical to the safe use of quizartinib. They both noted that a required REMS program that provided a brief overview of the novel mechanism of action would be essential to mitigate this potentially fatal risk. The REMS program should also educate the providers on the important factors common to TdP cases (females, severe hypokalemia, concomitant QT prolonging medications) in addition to appropriate management of quizartinib when these risks occur. They both noted that required REMS training for various products have become a requirement of attending on inpatient leukemia services and that these trainings have not been overly burdensome.
L2312: ## 10 Pediatrics
L2313: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2314: The Applicant and FDA reached agreement on an agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan (Ref. ID 4895995). Per this agreement, the Applicant is including in this application a waiver request for the conduct of studies in pediatric subjects &lt;1 month with *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML on the grounds that such studies would be highly impractical due to the rarity of *FLT3*-ITD mutation in this age group. Per the same agreement, the Applicant is including in this application a request for deferral of submission of results from agreed upon pediatric studies (ie, the study reports) of quizartinib for all ages \ge1 month because the drug product is anticipated to be ready for approval for use in adult subjects with *FLT3*-ITD (+) newly diagnosed AML before the clinical pediatric studies agreed upon are complete as provided for under 21 U.S.C. 355c(4)(A)(i)(I). Pursuant to the agreed upon initial Pediatric Study Plan, the Applicant is conducting Study AC220-A-U202 (NCT03793478) in addition to a modeling and extrapolation study to generate efficacy data from the adult newly diagnosed *FLT3*-ITD (+) AML Study AC220-A-U302 that is supporting this application.
L2315: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2316: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of their pediatric studies. Study AC220-A-U202 has been open for several years and has been slowly accruing patients. Despite being ongoing, the study has not yet achieved its objective to complete assessment of quizartinib in pediatric patients. Therefore, the Applicant's request for deferral is permissible. Moreover, FDA agrees that partial waiver for study of pediatric patients less than one year of age is also permissible. Lastly, completion and submission of a final CSR for Study AC220-A-U202 will be included as a PMR for quizartinib approval as detailed in section 13.

L2324: ## 12 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L2325: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2326: The Division of Risk Management (DRM) in the Office of Surveillance and Epidemiology (OSE) reviewed this Application and concurs with the review team that additional risk evaluation and mitigation strategies (REMS) are required to ensure the risks of quizartinib can be adequately managed in the post-marketing setting.
L2327: The specific goal of the REMS with elements to assure safe use (ETASU) is to mitigate the risk of
L2328: severe and fatal cardiac arrhythmias by ensuring that prescribers are able to identify the unique QT prolonging mechanism of quizartinib, risk factors associated with Torsades de Pointes and cardiac arrest with quizartinib, and the importance of providing risk mitigation measures including QTc interval monitoring, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, avoidance of concomitant QTc prolonging medications, and dose modification/dose interruptions when indicated.
L2329: Components of the REMS for quizartinib will include ETASU A (certification of prescribers) and ETASU B (certification of pharmacies that dispense quizartinib). Under ETASU A, prescribers must obtain certification by enrolling and completing training including a knowledge assessment regarding the risks of cardiac arrhythmias and agree to counsel patients on the risks and provide them with a patient wallet card. Under ETASU B, pharmacies must be certified and verify that prescribers are certified before dispensing quizartinib.
L2330: As part of the REMS, the Sponsor must submit REMS Assessments annually from the date of the initial approval. Depending on the findings from formal assessment of the REMS, FDA may modify the REMS or consider other regulatory actions. In the future, if the REMS assessments and/or data from other sources indicates that prescribers have gained familiarity with the risks of cardiac arrhythmias with quizartinib and are taking appropriate actions to reduce and manage the risks, FDA may re-evaluate the REMS to determine if continuation of REMS is necessary.
L2331: Refer to REMS review from DRM for additional details.
L2332: ## 13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
L2333: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2334: The following PMRs/PMCs will be issued to the Applicant:
L2335: - PMR #1: Conduct a clinical study to confirm the appropriate dose of quizartinib, and to assess safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of quizartinib in combination with fludarabine and cytarabine, in pediatric patients ages \ge 1 month to &lt;18 years with relapsed/refractory FLT3-ITD positive AML. Patients should be followed for at least 12 months (52 weeks). Include at least 16 patients \ge 1 month to &lt;18 years old, including a minimum of 4 patients &lt; 12 years old.
- PMR #2: Conduct an exercise test study in healthy subjects to further evaluate the serious risk of QTc prolongation with quizartinib. Evaluate the impact of rapid acceleration in heart rate on the cardiac safety of quizartinib using a standardized test protocol such as Bruce protocol, modified Bruce protocol, or graded-intensity bicycle exercise test. Identify QT/QTc and RR intervals at rest, peak exercise, and recovery, and capture the incidence of arrhythmias and other adverse reactions.
- PMR #3: Conduct a sub-maximal exercise test study in patients with AML to further evaluate the serious risk of QTc prolongation with quizartinib. Evaluate the impact of rapid acceleration in heart rate on the cardiac safety of quizartinib using a 24-Hour Holter study with sub-maximal exercise (e.g., postural provocation – i.e., supine to standing). Identify QT/QTc and RR intervals at rest and sub-maximal exercise and capture the incidence of arrhythmias and other adverse reactions. Include patients on beta-blockers in order to observe for mitigating effects on the incidence of quizartinib-related adverse reactions.
- PMR #4: Conduct a randomized trial of quizartinib maintenance therapy that compares the recommended dosage of 53 mg daily to a lower dosage (e.g., 26.5 mg daily) to further characterize serious adverse reactions including but not limited to the rates of Grade \ge 3 adverse reactions, Grade \ge 3 neutropenia, QTc interval prolongation, and dose reductions, interruptions, and discontinuations due to adverse reactions and provide an analysis of dose- and exposure-response relationships for safety. Incorporate systematically assessed patient-reported outcome assessments to further characterize safety and tolerability, including patient-reported symptoms, function, and overall side effect impact. Eligible patients will include newly diagnosed, FLT3-ITD positive AML patients in complete remission following consolidation and exclude patients who underwent allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. The study should also provide an analysis of dose- and exposure-response relationships for efficacy, including overall survival and relapse-free survival.
L2336: 5. PMR #5: Conduct an observational study using electronic health records (EHR) to further assess the risk of severe (Grades 3-4) and fatal ventricular arrhythmia events in adult patients treated with quizartinib for the indication of acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is FLT3-ITD positive as detected by an FDA-approved test. The selected EHR data source should contain access to clinical data elements including ECG results, laboratory results, concomitant medications, and clinical data to allow for outcome validation (i.e., via chart review). Evaluate the incidence of severe and fatal arrhythmia events and collect detailed clinical features of the adverse reactions, to investigate associations and temporal relationships between the incidence and severity of arrhythmia events and other potential associated risk factors. Specify case definitions, measurement, validation methods, and procedures for all study outcomes.

6. PMR #6: Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to assess the magnitude of change in exposure of quizartinib and its metabolite AC886 and to determine appropriate dosing recommendations of quizartinib in patients with severe hepatic impairment. Design and conduct the trial in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry: Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Hepatic Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling.

7. PMC #1: Conduct exploratory analyses aimed at identifying potential mechanisms of primary and acquired resistance to quizartinib using longitudinal samples for cytogenetic and mutational analyses collected at baseline and at the end of treatment or time of relapse from patients treated with quizartinib in QuANTUM-First. Include a discussion of the results in the context of the available published literature.

8. PMC #2: Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to assess the magnitude of decreased exposure of quizartinib and its metabolite AC886, and to determine appropriate dosing recommendations for quizartinib, with coadministration of a weak CYP3A inducer. Design and conduct the trial in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry: Clinical Drug Interaction Studies – Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions.

L2425: ## 19.4. OCP Appendices (Technical Documents Supporting OCP Recommendations)
L2426: ## 19.4.1. Bioanalytical Methods Section
L2427: In the AC220-A-U302 trial, a validated HPLC/MS/MS methods was used to quantitate quizartinib and its major, active, metabolite AC886. The method is summarized below.
L2428: Bioanalytical method validation report name, amendments, and hyperlinks	Quantification of quizartinib (AC220) and AC886 in K2EDTA human plasma by LC-MS/MS (Module 5.3.1.4 Report 1000-141851-1)
Long-term stability experiment of quizartinib (AC220) and its metabolite AC886 in human plasma (Module 5.3.1.4 Report 1000-141851-1 Addendum 1)
Additional long-term stability, additional matrix factor experiment, Selectivity towards Lipemic Plasma, Selectivity towards Co-administered Drugs, Stability Program with Co- administered Drug and Additional AC886-d4 Stock Stability (Module 5.3.1.4 Report 1000-141851-1 Addendum 2)
Method description	Quizartinib (AC220) and AC886 were quantitatively measured from human K2 EDTA plasma using an LC-MS/MS method. Plasma samples containing analytes were mixed with deuterated internal standards (IS) and deproteinated by organic solvents and filtered, followed by evaporation to dryness of the eluate and then reconstitution of the residue. Extracted samples were analyzed by reverse-phase chromatography with detection using a triple quadrupole mass spectrometer with an electrospray ion source operated in positive ionization mode.
Materials used for calibration curve & concentration	Matrix: Human K2EDTA plasma
Analytes: Quizartinib, AC886
Internal Standards: quizartinib-d4, AC886-d4
Calibration Curve concentrations: 0.500 (LLOQ), 1.00, 5.00, 25.0, 150, 300, 450, and 500 ng/mL (ULOQ)
Validated assay range	0.500 ng/mL to 500 ng/mL
Material used for QCs & concentration	Matrix: Human K2 EDTA plasma
Analytes: Quizartinib, AC886
Internal Standards: quizartinib-d4, AC886-d4
QC Samples: 1.50 ng/mL, 50.0 ng/mL, 250 ng/mL, 400 ng/mL
L2429: Minimum required dilutions	Minimum required dilution (MRD) is not applicable for small molecule LC-MS/MS.
Source & lot of reagents (LBA)	Supplier	Quizartinib/Quizartinib-d4 (IS)	AC886/AC886-d4 (IS)
Supplier	Quizartinib/Quizartinib-d4 (IS)	AC886/AC886-d4 (IS)
Source & lot of reagents (LBA)	Lot No.	HQ00005/11-00181-01	006/11-00182-01

Regression model & weighting	Linear regression (y = ax+b) with 1/x^2 weighting factor
Validation parameters	Method validation summary
Calibration curve performance during accuracy & precision	Number of calibration standards from LLOQ to ULOQ for both analytes	8
